A meta-theory for big-step semantics DOI: 10.1145/1122445.1122456 Journal: TOCL Volume: 37 4 111 8 CCS: Theory of computation Operational semantics
Francesco Dagnino

Abstract
. It is well-known that big-step semantics is not able to distinguish stuck and non-terminating computations. This is a strong limitation as it makes very difficult to reason about properties involving infinite computations, such as type soundness, which cannot even be expressed. We show that this issue is only apparent: the distinction between stuck and diverging computations is implicit in any big-step semantics and it just needs to be uncovered. To achieve this goal, we develop a systematic study of big-step semantics: we introduce an abstract definition of what a big-step semantics is, we define a notion of computation by formalising the evaluation algorithm implicitly associated with any big-step semantics, and we show how to canonically extend a big-step semantics to characterise stuck and diverging computations. Building on these notions, we describe a general proof technique to show that a predicate is sound, that is, it prevents stuck computation, with respect to a big-step semantics. One needs to check three properties relating the predicate and the semantics and, if they hold, the predicate is sound. The extended semantics are essential to establish this meta-logical result, but are of no concerns to the user, who only needs to prove the three properties of the initial big-step semantics. Finally, we illustrate the technique by several examples, showing that it is applicable also in cases where subject reduction does not hold, hence the standard technique for small-step semantics cannot be used.



1.
Introduction

The operational semantics of programming languages or software systems specifies, for each program/system configuration, its final result, if any.
In the case of non-existence of a final result, there are two possibilities:

•

either the computation stops with no final result:
stuck computation
,

•

or the computation never stops:
non-termination
.

There are two main styles to define operationally a semantic relation: the
small-step
style
(
Plotkin 1981
;
Plotkin 2004
)
, on top of a transition relation representing single computation steps, or directly by a set of rules as in the
big-step
style
(
Kahn 1987
)
.
Within a small-step semantics it is straightforward to make the distinction between stuck and non-terminating computations, while a typical drawback of the big-step style is that they are not distinguished (no judgement is derived in both cases).
Actually, in big-step style, it is not even clear
what a computation is
, because the only available notion is derivability of judgements, which does not convey the dynamics of computation.

For this reason, even though big-step semantics is generally more abstract, and sometimes more intuitive to design and therefore to debug and extend, in the literature much more effort has been devoted to study the meta-theory of small-step semantics, providing properties, and related proof techniques. Notably, the
soundness
of a type system (typing prevents stuck computation) can be proved by
progress
and
subject reduction
, also called
type preservation
,
(
Wright and
Felleisen 1994
)
.
Note that soundness cannot even be
expressed
with respect to a big-step semantics, since non-termination and stuckness are confused, as they are both modelled by the absence of a final result.

Our quest in this paper is to develop a meta-theory of big-step operational semantics, to enable formal reasoning also on non-terminating computations.
More precisely, we will address the following problems:

(1)

Defining, in a formal way,
computations
in a given arbitrary big-step semantics.

(2)

According to this definition, describing
extensions of a given arbitrary big-step semantics
, where the difference between stuckness and non-termination is made explicit.

(3)

Providing a general proof technique by identifying
three sufficient conditions
on the original big-step rules to prove soundness of a predicate.

All these points rely on the same fundamental cornerstone:
a general definition of big-step semantics.
Such a definition captures the essential features of a big-step semantics, independently from the particular language or system.

To address
Item

1
, we rely on the intuition that every big-step semantics implicitly defines an evaluation algorithm.
Then, we identify computations in the big-step semantics with computations of such algorithm.
Formally, we extend the big-step semantics to model
partial evaluations
, representing intermediate states of the evaluation process,
and we formalise the evaluation algorithm by a transition relation between such intermediate states.
Then, computations are just sequences of transition steps.
Note that the use of a transition relation is somehow necessary to define computations since they are related to the dynamics of the evaluation and it cannot be captured by derivability in big-step semantics, as it is too abstract.
In this way, we get a reference model of computations in big-step semantics,
where we can easily distinguish stuck and non-terminating computations, thus showing that this distinction is actually present, but hidden, in any big-step semantics.

To deal with
Item

2
, we describe extensions of a given big-step semantics capable to distinguish between stuck and non-terminating computations, as defined in
Item

1
, but abstracting away single computation steps.
In this way, we show that such a distinction can be made directly in a big-step style.
More in detail,
starting from an arbitrary big-step judgment
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
that evaluates
configurations

\mathit{c}
into
results

\mathit{r}
, the first construction produces an enriched judgement
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
where
\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}
is either a pair
\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle
consisting of a finite trace
\mathit{t}
and a result
\mathit{r}
, or an infinite trace
\sigma
.
Finite and infinite traces model the (finite or infinite) sequences of all the configurations encountered during the evaluation.
In this way, by interpreting coinductively the rules of the extended semantics, an infinite trace models divergence (whereas no result corresponds to stuck computation).
Furthermore, we will show that, by using
coaxioms

(
Ancona
et al. 2017a
;
Dagnino 2019
)
, we can get rid of traces, modelling divergence just by a judgmeent
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.
The second construction is in a sense dual.
It is the general version of the well-known technique presented in Exercise 3.5.16 by
Pierce 2002
of adding a special result
wrong
explicitly modelling stuck computations (whereas no result corresponds to divergence).
We will show that these constructions are correct, proving that they represent the intended class of computations as defined in
Item

1
.

Three sufficient conditions in
Item

3
are
local preservation
,
\exists
-progress
, and
\forall
-progress
.
For
proving
the result that the three conditions actually ensure soundness, we crucially rely on the extended big-step semantics of
Item

2
, since otherwise, as said above, we could not even express the property.

However, the three conditions deal only with the original rules of the given big-step semantics.

This means that, practically, in order to use the technique there is no need to deal with the meta-theory (computations and extended semantics).
This implies, in particular, that our approach does
not
increase the original number of rules.
Moreover, the sufficient conditions are checked only on
single rules
, hence neither induction nor coinduction is needed.
In a sense,
they make explicit elementary fragments of the soundness proof, embedding such semantic-dependent fragments in a semantic-independent (co)inductive proof, which we carry out once and for all (cf.
Theorems

7.6
and
7.9
).

We support our approach by presenting several examples, demonstrating that: on the one hand, soundness proofs can be easily rephrased in terms of our technique, that is, by directly reasoning on big-step rules;
on the other hand, our technique works also when the property to be checked (for instance, well-typedness) is
not preserved
by intermediate computation steps, whereas it holds for the final result.
On a side note, our examples concern type systems, but the meta-theory we present in this work holds for any predicate.

Actually,
we can express two flavours of soundness, depending on whether we make explicit stuckness or non-termination.
In the former case we express
soundness-must
, which is the notion of soundness we have considered so far, preventing all stuck computations, while
in the latter case we express
soundness-may
, a weaker notion only ensuring
the existence of
a non-stuck computation.
Of course, this distinction is relevant only in presence of non-determinism, otherwise the two notions coincide.
We define a proof technique for soundness-may as well, showing it is correct.
In the end, it should be noted that
we define soundness with respect to a big-step semantics within a big-step formulation
, without resorting to a small-step style (indeed, the extended semantics are themselves big-step).

This paper is extracted from the PhD thesis of the author
(
Dagnino 2021a
)
and extends the work presented in
Dagnino et al. 2020b
in several ways:
first, we consider a more natural and general notion of big-step semantics;
we provide a detailed analysis of computations in big-step semantics;
we define an additional construction based on coaxioms generalising the approach in
Ancona
et al. 2017b
;
finally, we improve examples considering also imperative languages.

The rest of the paper is organised as follows.

Section

2
recalls basic notions about inference systems and corules.

Section

3
provides a definition of big-step semantics.

Section

4

defines computations in big-step semantics as possibly infinite sequences of steps in a transition relation on partial evaluation trees. In this way we get a reference semantic model.

Section

5
defines two constructions extending a given big-step semantics:
one, based on traces, which explicitly models diverging computations and another which explicitly models stuck computations.

Section

6
defines a third construction, modelling divergence just as a special result, by using appropriate corules.

Section

7
shows how we can express two flavours of soundness against big-step semantics and provides proof techniques to show this property.

Section

8
illustrates the proof technique on several examples.
Finally,
Section

9
concludes the paper, discussing related and future work.

2.
Preliminaries on inference systems and corules

In this section, we recall standard notions about (co)inductive definitions by inference systems
(
Aczel 1977
;
Leroy and Grall 2009
;
Sangiorgi 2011
)
, which are used throughout the paper, and
also their generalisation by corules, introduced by
Ancona
et al. 2017a
;
Dagnino 2019
;
Dagnino 2021a
,
which enable more flexible coinductive definitions.
Corules will be only used in
Sections

6
and
7
to properly model and reason about diverging computations in a big-step semantics.

Assume a set
\mathcal{U}
, named
universe
, whose elements are called
judgements
.
An
inference system

\mathcal{I}

is a set of
(inference) rules
, which are pairs
{\langle{\mathit{Pr},\mathit{c}}\rangle}
, where
\mathit{Pr}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
the set of
premises
and
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{U}
the
conclusion
(a.k.a.
consequence
).
As it is customary, rules are often written as fractions
\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{Pr}}{\mathit{c}}\
.
A rule with an empty set of premises is an
axiom
.
A
proof tree
(a.k.a.
derivation
) for a judgement
\mathit{j}
in
\mathcal{I}
is a tree whose nodes are (labeled with) judgements in
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathit{j}
is the root, and there is a node
\mathit{c}
with set of children
\mathit{Pr}
only if there is a rule
{\langle{\mathit{Pr},\mathit{c}}\rangle}
in
\mathcal{I}
.
The
inductive
and the
coinductive interpretations
of
\mathcal{I}
, denoted
\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket
and
\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket
, respectively, are the sets of judgements with, respectively a well-founded
1
1

1

It is finite when sets of premises are finite.
and an arbitrary (well-founded or not) proof tree.
We will write
\mathcal{I}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}
and
\mathcal{I}\vdash_{\nu}\mathit{j}
when
\mathit{j}\in\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket
and
\mathit{j}\in\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket
, respectively.
Set-theoretically, we say that a subset
X\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is

(
\mathcal{I}
-)closed
if, for every rule
{\langle{\mathit{Pr},\mathit{j}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{I}
,
\mathit{Pr}\subseteq X
implies
\mathit{j}\in X
, and

(
\mathcal{I}
-)consistent
if, for every
\mathit{j}\in X
, there is a rule
{\langle{\mathit{Pr},\mathit{j}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathit{Pr}\subseteq X
.
Then, it can be proved that
\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket
is the least closed subset and
\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket
is the largest consistent subset and this provides us with the following proof principles:

induction principle:

if
X\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is closed then
\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket\subseteq X

coinduction principle:

if
X\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is consistent then
X\subseteq\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\rrbracket

We recall now the notion of inference system with corules
(
Ancona
et al. 2017a
;
Dagnino 2019
;
Dagnino 2021a
)
,
which mixes induction and coinduction in a specific way.

For a set
X\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, let

\mathcal{I}_{\mid X}
denote the inference system obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by keeping only rules with conclusion in
X
.

Definition 2.1 (Inference system with corules).

An
inference system with corules
, or
generalised inference system
, is a pair
{\langle{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}
where
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}
are inference systems, whose elements are called
rules
and
corules
, respectively.
A corule with empty set of premises is a
coaxiom
.
The
interpretation

\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
of such a pair is defined by

\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket=\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I}_{\mid\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket}\rrbracket
.

Thus, the interpretation
\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
is basically
coinductive
, but restricted to
a universe of judgements which is
inductively defined
by the (potentially) larger system
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}
.
In proof-theoretic terms,
\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
is the set of judgements which have an arbitrary (well-founded or not) proof tree in
\mathcal{I}
whose nodes all have a well-founded proof tree in
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}
, that is, the (standard) inference system consisting of both rules and corules.
We will write
{\langle{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}\mathit{j}
when
\mathit{j}
is derivable in
{\langle{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}
, that is,
\mathit{j}\in\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
.

We illustrate these notions by a simple example.
As usual, sets of rules are expressed by
meta-rules
with side conditions, and analogously sets of corules are expressed by
meta-corules
with side conditions.
(Meta-)corules will be written with thicker lines, to be distinguished from (meta-)rules.
The following inference system defines the maximal element of a list of natural numbers, where
\varepsilon
is the empty list, and
\mathit{x}{:}u
the list with head
\mathit{x}
and tail
u
.

\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathsf{maxElem}(x{:}\varepsilon,x)}\ \hskip 15.00002pt\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{maxElem}(u,y)}{\mathsf{maxElem}(x{:}u,z)}\ z=\max(x,y)

The inductive interpretation is defined only on finite lists, since for infinite lists an infinite proof is needed.
However, the coinductive interpretation allows the derivation of wrong judgements.
For instance, let
L=1:2:1:2:1:2:\ldots
.
Then, any judgement
\mathsf{maxElem}(L,x)
with
x\geq 2
can be derived, as illustrated by the following examples.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\ldots}{\mathsf{maxElem}(L,2)}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(2{:}L,2)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,2)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\hskip 15.00002pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\ldots}{\mathsf{maxElem}(L,5)}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(2{:}L,5)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,5)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}

By adding a corule (in this case a coaxiom), we add a constraint which forces the greatest element
to belong to the list, so that wrong results are “filtered out”:

\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathsf{maxElem}(x{:}\varepsilon,x)}\ \hskip 15.00002pt\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{maxElem}(u,y)}{\mathsf{maxElem}(x{:}u,z)}\ z=\max(x,y)\hskip 15.00002pt\genfrac{}{}{1.5pt}{0}{}{\maxElem{x\cons u}{x}}

Indeed, the judgement
\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,2)
has the infinite proof tree shown above, and each node has a finite proof tree in the inference system extended by the corule:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\ldots}{\mathsf{maxElem}(L,2)}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(2{:}L,2)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,2)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\hskip 15.00002pt\frac{\frac{}{\mathsf{maxElem}(2{:}L,2)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\genfrac{}{}{1.5pt}{0}{,}{l}$}eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,2)}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}

On the other hand, the judgement
\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,5)
has the infinite proof tree shown above, but has
no finite proof tree
in the inference system extended by the corule. Indeed, since
5
does not belong to the list, the corule can never be applied. Hence, this judgement cannot be derived in the inference system with corules.
Finally, note that the judgement
\mathsf{maxElem}(1{:}2{:}L,1)
has a finite proof tree in the inference system extended by the corule, but has no proof tree in the system with no corules, as
1
is not an upper bound of the list.
We refer to
(
Ancona
et al. 2017a
;
Ancona
et al. 2017b
;
Ancona
et al. 2018
;
Dagnino 2019
;
Dagnino
et al. 2020a
;
Dagnino 2021a
)
for other examples.

Let
{\langle{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}
be a generalised inference system.
The interpretation
\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
can be characterised as the largest
\mathcal{I}
-consistent subset of
\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
, and this provides us with
the
bounded coinduction principle
, a generalisation of the standard coinduction principle.

Theorem 2.2 (Bounded coinduction).

Let
X\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.
If
X
is
\mathcal{I}
-consistent and
{X\subseteq\mu\llbracket\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket}
,
then
X\subseteq\nu\llbracket\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}\rrbracket
.

In other words, to prove that every judgement in
X
is derivable in
\langle{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle
,
we have to prove that
every judgement in
X
has a well-founded proof tree in
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{co}}
and
every judgement in
X
is the conclusion of a rule whose premises are all in
X
.

3.
Defining big-step semantics

As mentioned in the introduction, the corner stone of this paper is
a formalisation of what a big-step semantics is, that captures its essential features, subsuming
a large class of examples. This enables a general formal reasoning on an arbitrary big-step semantics.

Definition 3.1.

A
big-step semantics
is a triple
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
where:

•

\mathit{C}
is a set of
configurations

\mathit{c}
.

•

\mathit{R}
is a set of
results

\mathit{r}
.
A
judgment

\mathit{j}
is a pair written
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, meaning that configuration
\mathit{c}
evaluates to result
\mathit{r}
.
Set
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j})=\mathit{c}
and
\mathit{R}(\mathit{j})=\mathit{r}
.

•

\mathcal{R}
is a set of
(big-step) rules

\rho
of shape

\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{j}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathit{j}_{n}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}}\

also written in
inline format
:
{\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})

where
\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n}
, with
n\geq 0
, is a sequence of premises.
Set
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
,
\mathit{R}(\rho)=\mathit{r}
and, for
i\in 1..n
,
\mathit{C}(\rho,i)=\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})
and
\mathit{R}(\rho,i)=\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{i})
.

We require
\mathcal{R}
to satisfy the
bounded premises
condition:

BP
:

for every
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}
, there exists
b_{\mathit{c}}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for each
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
,
n\leq b_{\mathit{c}}
.

We will use the inline format, more concise and manageable, for the development of the meta-theory, e.g., in constructions.

Big-step rules, as defined above, are very much like inference rules (cf.
Section

2
),
but they carry slightly more structure with respect to them.
Notably, premises are a sequence rather than a set, that is, they are ordered and there can be repeated premises.
Such additional structure, however, does not affect derivability, namely, the inference operator and so the interpretations of such rules.
Therefore, given a big-step semantics
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
, slightly abusing the notation,
we denote by
\mathcal{R}
the inference system obtained by forgetting such additional structure, and
define, as usual, the
semantic relation
as the inductive interpretation of
\mathcal{R}
.
Then, we write
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
when the judgment
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
is derivable in
\mathcal{R}
.

Even though the additional structure of big-step rules does not affect the semantic relation they define,
it is crucial to develop the meta-theory, allowing abstract reasoning about an arbitrary big-step semantics.
It will be used in all results in this paper: to define computations in big-step semantics, then to provide constructions yelding extended semantics able to distinguish stuck and diverging computations and, finally, to define proof techniques for soundness.
Indeed, as premises are a sequence, we know in which order configurations in the premises should be evaluated.

In practice, the (infinite) set of rules
\mathcal{R}
is described by a finite set of meta-rules, each one with a finite number of premises.
As a consequence, for each configuration, the number of premises of rules with such a configuration in the conclusion is not only finite but
bounded
.
Since we have no notion of meta-rule, we explicitly require this feature (relevant in the following) by the bounded premises (
BP
) condition.

We end this section by illustrating the above definitions and conditions on a simple example: a
\lambda
-calculus with constants for natural numbers, successor and non-deterministic choice, shown in
Fig.

1
.
We denote by
\mathit{x}
variables and by
\mathit{n}
natural number constants.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{e}&::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{v}\mid\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}\mid\texttt{succ}\,e\mid\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}&\text{expression}\\
\mathit{v}&::=&\mathit{n}\mid\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}&\text{value}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(val)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{v}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\textsc{\scriptsize{( app )}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda x.e}\hskip 9.24994pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(succ)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{n}}}{{\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{n}+1}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 6.47491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(choice)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}i=1,2\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({val})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}(\varepsilon,\,\mathit{v},\,\mathit{v})\\
{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({app})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\ \ {\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\ \ {{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}},\,\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2},\,\mathit{v})\\
{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({succ})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{n}},\,\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e},\,\mathit{n}+1)\\
{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({choice})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}},\,\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2},\,\mathit{v})\ i=1,2\end{array}

Figure 1.
Example of big-step semantics

It is immediate to see this example as an instance of
Definition

3.1
:

•

Configurations and results are expressions, and values, respectively.
2
2

2

In general, configurations may include additional components and results are not necessarily particular configurations, see,
e.g.
,
Section

8.2
.

•

To have the set of (meta-)rules in our required shape, abbreviated in inline format in the bottom section of the figure, we have only to assume an order on premises of rule
(app)
.

Remark 3.2.

The order of premises chosen for rule
(app)
in
Fig.

1
formalises the evaluation strategy for
an application
\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}
where
first (1) evaluates
\mathit{e}_{1}
, then (2) checks that the value of
\mathit{e}_{1}
is a
\lambda
-abstraction, finally (3) evaluates
\mathit{e}_{2}
.
That is, left-to-right evaluation with early error detection.
Other strategies can be obtained by choosing a different order or by adjusting big-step rules.
Notably, right-to-left evaluation (3)-(1)-(2) can be expressed by just swapping the first two premises, that is:

{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({app-r})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\ \ {\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\ \ {{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}},\,\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2},\,\mathit{v})

Left-to-right evaluation with late error detection (1)-(3)-(2) can be expressed as follows:

{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({app-late})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{1}}\ \ {\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\ \ {\mathit{v}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\ \ {{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}},\,\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2},\,\mathit{v})

We can even opt for a non-deterministic approach by taking more than one rule among
(app)
,
(app-r)
and
(app-late)
.
As said above, these different choices do not affect the semantic relation inductively defined by the inference system, which is always the same.
However, they will affect computations and thus the extended semantics distinguishing stuck computation and non-termination.
Indeed, if the evaluation of
\mathit{e}_{1}
and
\mathit{e}_{2}
is stuck and non-terminating, respectively, we should obtain a stuck computation with rule
(app)
and non-termination with rule
(app-r)
;
further, if
\mathit{e}_{1}
evaluates to a natural constant and
\mathit{e}_{2}
diverges, we should obtain a stuck computation with rule
(app)
and non-termination with rule
(app-late)
.

In summary, to see a typical big-step semantics as an instance of our definition, it is enough to identify configurations and results and to assume an order (or more than one) on premises.

4.
Computations in big-step semantics

Intuitively, the evaluation of a configuration
\mathit{c}
is a
dynamic
process and, as such, it may either successfully terminate producing the final result, or get stuck, or never terminate.
However, a big-step semantics just tells us whether a configuration
\mathit{c}
evaluates to a certain result
\mathit{r}
, without describing the dynamics of such evaluation process.
This is nice, because it allows us to abstract away details about intermediate states in the evaluation process, but it makes quite difficult to reason about concepts like non-termination and stuckness, since they refer to computations and we do not even know what a computation is in a big-step semantics.

In this section, we show that, given a big-step semantics as defined in
Definition

3.1
,
we can recover the dynamics of the evaluation, by defining
computations
, which, in a sense, are implicit in a big-step specification.
To this end, we extend the big-step semantics, so that we can represent partial (or incomplete) evaluations, modelling intermediate states of the evaluation process.
Then, we model the dynamics by a transition relation beween such partial evaluations,
hence, as usual, a computation will be a (possibly infinite) sequence of transitions.

Let us assume a big-step semantics
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
.
As said above, the first step is to extend such semantics to model partial evaluations.
To this end, first of all, we introduce a special result
?
, so that a judgment
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
(called
incomplete
, whereas a judgment
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
is
complete
) means that the evaluation of
\mathit{c}
is not completed yet.
Set
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}=\mathit{R}+\{\textsf{?}\}
whose elements are ranged over by
\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}}
.
We now define an augmented set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
to properly handle the new result
?
:

Definition 4.1 (Rules for partial evaluation).

The set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
is obtained from
\mathcal{R}
by adding the following rules:

start rules:

For each configuration
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}
, define rule
{\mathsf{ax}_{\textsf{?}}}(\mathit{c})
as
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
.

partial rules:

For each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
, index
i\in 1..n
, and
\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}}\in\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}
,
define rule
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}})
as

\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{j}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathit{j}_{i-1}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\

Intuitively, start rules allow us to begin the evaluation of any configuration, while
partial rules allow us to partially apply a rule from
\mathcal{R}
to derive a partial judgement.
Note that the last premise of a partial rule can be either complete (
\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}}\in\mathit{R}
) or incomplete (
\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}}=\textsf{?}
), in the latter case we also call it a
?
-propagation
rule, since it propagates
?
from premises to the conclusion.

It is important to observe that the construction described above yields
a triple
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}},\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}}\rangle}
, which is a big-step semantics according to
Definition

3.1
.

3
3

3

The condition (
BP
) is satisfied as the number of premises of the additional rules is bounded by that of a rule in the original semantics.

In
Fig.

2
we report rules added by the construction in
Definition

4.1
to the big-step semantics of the
\lambda
-calculus in
Fig.

1
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \hskip 15.00002pt\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}{{\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \hskip 15.00002pt\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \ i=1,2\\[12.91663pt]
\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \par\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \par\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\hskip 6.99997pt{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\end{array}

Figure 2.
Rules for
?
for the
\lambda
-calculus in
Fig.

1
.

Given a big-step semantics
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
,
using rules in
\mathcal{R}
, we can build trees called
evaluation trees
.
Such trees are very much like proof trees for an inference system, with the only difference that evaluation trees are
ordered
trees, because premises of big-step rules are a sequence.
Roughly, an evaluation tree is an ordered tree with nodes labelled by semantic judgements, such that for each node labelled by
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
with sequence of children
\mathit{j}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{j}_{n}
, there is a rule
{\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
.

An evaluation tree for
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}},\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}}\rangle}
is called a
partial evaluation tree
, as it can contain incomplete judgements.
We say that a partial evaluation tree is
complete
if it only contains complete judgments, it is
incomplete
otherwise.
Finite partial evaluation trees indeed model possibly incomplete evaluation of configurations, namely, the intermediate states of the evaluation process, because big-step rules can be partially applied.
Hence, they are the fundamental building block, which will allow us to define computations in big-step semantics.

In the next subsection we will give a formal definition of (partial) evaluation trees, similar to that of proof trees
(
Dagnino 2019
;
Dagnino 2021b
;
Dagnino 2021a
)
.
This formal definition is needed to state some results and to carry out proofs in a rigorous way, and it is not essential to follow the rest of the paper, hence the reader not interested in formal details can skip it, relying on the above semiformal definition.

4.1.
The structure of partial evaluation trees

We give a formal account of (partial) evaluation trees, which is useful to state and prove technical results in the next sections.
This development is based on the definition and properties of trees provided by
Courcelle 1983
, adjusted to our specific setting.

Let
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
be the set of positive natural numbers,
\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
the set of finite sequences of positive natural numbers and
\mathcal{L}
a set of labels.
An
ordered tree
labelled in
\mathcal{L}
is a partial function
{\tau}:{\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}
such that

\textsf{dom}(\tau)
is not empty, and, for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
if
\alpha n\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
then

\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
and, for all
k\leq n
,
\alpha k\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
.
Given an ordered tree
\tau
and
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
, set
\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)=\sup\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid\alpha n\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)\}
the
branching
of
\tau
at
\alpha
, and

{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
the
subtree
of
\tau
rooted at
\alpha
, that is,
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}(\beta)=\tau(\alpha\beta)
, for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
.
The
root
of
\tau
is
\mathsf{r}(\tau)=\tau(\varepsilon)
and obviously we have
\tau={\tau}_{\mid_{\varepsilon}}
.
Finally, we write
\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau_{n}}{x}\
for the tree
\tau
defined by
\tau(\varepsilon)=x
, and
\tau(i\alpha)=\tau_{i}(\alpha)
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Since in the following we will only deal with ordered trees, we will refer to them just as trees.

Let us assume a big-step semantics
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
.
Assume also that labels in
\mathcal{L}
are semantic judgments
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
,
then we can define evaluation trees as follows:

Definition 4.2.

A tree
{\tau}:{\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}
is an
evaluation tree
in
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
,
if, for each
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
with
\tau(\alpha)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, there is
{\mathsf{rule}}(\tau(\alpha 1)\ldots\tau(\alpha\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)),\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Note that, starting from an evaluation tree
\tau
, we can construct a proof tree for the inference system denoted by
\mathcal{R}
, by forgetting the order on sibling nodes and removing duplicated children.
Therefore, if
\tau
is a finite evaluation tree with
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, then
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
holds.

Definition 4.3.

A
partial evaluation tree
in
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
is an evaluation tree in
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}},\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}}\rangle}
.

The following proposition assures two key properties of partial evaluation trees.
First, if there is some
?
, then it is propagated to ancestor nodes.
Second, for each level of the tree there is at most one
?
. We set
\left|\alpha\right|
the length of
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
.

Proposition 4.4.

Let
\tau
be a partial evaluation tree, then the following hold:

(1)

for all
\alpha n\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
, if
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha n))=\textsf{?}
then
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))=\textsf{?}
.

(2)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is at most one
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
with
\left|\alpha\right|=n
such that
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))=\textsf{?}
.

Proof.

To prove
Item

1
, we just have to note that the only rules having a premise
\mathit{j}
with
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathit{j})=\textsf{?}
are
?
-propagation rules, which also have conclusion
\mathit{j}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathit{j}^{\prime})=\textsf{?}
; hence the thesis is immediate.
To prove
Item

2
, we proceed by induction on
n
.
For
n=0
, there is only one
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
with
\left|\alpha\right|=0
(the empty sequence), hence the thesis is trivial.
Consider
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}k\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
with
\left|\alpha\right|=n+1
.
If
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))=\textsf{?}
, then, by
Item

1
,
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha^{\prime}))=\textsf{?}
, and, by induction hypothesis,
\alpha^{\prime}
is the only sequence of length
n
in
\textsf{dom}(\tau)
with this property.
Therefore, another node
\beta\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
, with
\left|\beta\right|=n+1
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\beta))=\textsf{?}
, must satisfy
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}h
for some
h\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
;
hence, since
\tau
is a partial evaluation tree,
\tau(\alpha)
and
\tau(\beta)
are two premises of the same rule with
?
as result, thus they must coincide, since all rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
have at most one premise with
?
.
∎

Corollary 4.5.

Let
\tau
be a partial evaluation tree, then

\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))\in\mathit{R}
if and only if
\tau
is complete.

We can define a relation
4
4

4

This is a slight variation of similar relations on trees considered by
Courcelle 1983
;
Dagnino 2019
.
, denoted by
\sqsubseteq
, on trees labelled by possibly incomplete judgements, as follows:

Definition 4.6.

Let
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
be trees labelled by possibly incomplete semantic judgements.
Define
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
if and only if

\textsf{dom}(\tau)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\prime})
and,
for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
,
\mathit{C}(\tau(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau^{\prime}(\alpha))
and

\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))\in\mathit{R}
implies
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau^{\prime}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
.

Intuitively,
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
means that
\tau^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\tau
by adding new branches or replacing some
?
s with results.
We use
\sqsubset
for the strict version of
\sqsubseteq
.
Note that, if
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
, then, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
,
\tau^{\prime}(\alpha)
is
more defined
than
\tau(\alpha)
, because,
either
\tau(\alpha)
is undefined, or
\tau(\alpha)
is incomplete and
\mathit{C}(\tau(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau^{\prime}(\alpha))
, or
\tau(\alpha)=\tau^{\prime}(\alpha)
.

It is easy to check that
\sqsubseteq
is a partial order and, if
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
, then, for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
,
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq{\tau^{\prime}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
.
The following proposition shows some, less trivial, properties of
\sqsubseteq
.

Proposition 4.7.

The following properties hold:

(1)

for all trees
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, if
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))\in\mathit{R}
, then
\tau=\tau^{\prime}

(2)

for each increasing sequence
(\tau_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of trees, there is a least upper bound
\tau=\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
.

Proof.

Item

1
is immediate by definition of
\sqsubseteq
.
To prove
Item

2
, first note that, since for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau_{n}\sqsubseteq\tau_{n+1}
,
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
we have that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is defined, then,
for all
k\geq n
,
\mathit{C}(\tau_{k}(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau_{n}(\alpha))
, and,
if
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau_{n}(\alpha))\in\mathit{R}
, then
\tau_{k}(\alpha)=\tau_{n}(\alpha)
.
Hence, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there are only three possibilities for
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
: it is either undefined, or equal to
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
, or equal to
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, where
\mathit{c}
and
\mathit{r}
are always the same.
Let us denote by
k_{\alpha}
the least index
n
where
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is most defined,
that is,
if
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is always undefined, then
k_{\alpha}=0
,
if
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is eventually always equal to
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
, then
k_{\alpha}
is the least
n
where
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is defined, and,
if
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is eventually always equal to
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, then
k_{\alpha}
is the least
n
where
\tau_{n}(\alpha)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Therefore, for all
n\geq k_{\alpha}
, we have that
\tau_{n}(\alpha)=\tau_{k_{\alpha}}(\alpha)
.

Consider a tree
\tau
defined by
\tau(\alpha)=\tau_{k_{\alpha}}(\alpha)
.
It is easy to check that
\textsf{dom}(\tau)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})
.
We now check that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau_{n}\sqsubseteq\tau
.
For all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})
, we have
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
and we distinguish two cases:

•

if
\tau_{n}(\alpha)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
, then, since either
\tau_{n}\sqsubseteq\tau_{k_{\alpha}}
or
\tau_{k_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq\tau_{n}
and
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau_{k_{\alpha}})
, we get
\mathit{C}(\tau(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau_{k_{\alpha}}(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau_{n}(\alpha))=\mathit{c}
;

•

if
\tau_{n}(\alpha)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, then
k_{\alpha}\leq n
, hence, since
\tau_{k_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq\tau_{n}
, we get
\mathit{C}(\tau(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau_{k_{\alpha}}(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau_{n}(\alpha))=\mathit{c}
, thus we have only to check that
{\tau_{n}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
.
To prove this point,
consider
\beta\in\textsf{dom}({\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}})
,
then, by
Corollary

4.5
,
we have
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}(\beta)=\tau(\alpha\beta)={\mathit{c}^{\prime}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}
,
hence, since for all
h\geq k_{\alpha}
we have
{\tau_{k_{\alpha}}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau_{h}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
, we get
\alpha\beta\in\textsf{dom}(\tau_{h})
and
{\tau_{h}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
is complete, thus
k_{\alpha\beta}\leq k_{\alpha}
.
Therefore,
\tau_{k_{\alpha\beta}}\sqsubseteq\tau_{k_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq\tau_{n}
and so we get
{\tau_{k_{\alpha\beta}}}_{\mid_{\alpha\beta}}={\tau_{n}}_{\mid_{\alpha\beta}}
, which implies that

{\tau_{n}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}(\beta)=\tau_{k_{\alpha\beta}}(\alpha\beta)=\tau(\alpha\beta)
, as needed.

This proves that
\tau
is an upper bound of the sequence, we have still to prove that it is the least one.
To this end, let
\tau^{\prime}
be an upper bound of the sequence: we have to show that
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
.
Since
\tau^{\prime}
is an upper bound, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\prime})
, hence
\textsf{dom}(\tau)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\prime})
, and, especially, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{\star}
we have
\tau_{k_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
.
Hence, for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
, we have
\mathit{C}(\tau(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau_{k_{\alpha}}(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau^{\prime}(\alpha))
, and, if
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))=\mathit{r}
, since
\tau_{k_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq\tau
and
\tau_{k_{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
, we have
{\tau_{k_{\alpha}}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
and
{\tau_{k_{\alpha}}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau^{\prime}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
, hence
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau^{\prime}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
, as needed.
∎

Obviously, this relation restricts to partial evaluation trees and, more importantly, the set of partial evaluation trees is closed with respect to least upper bound for
\sqsubseteq
, as the next proposition shows.

Proposition 4.8.

For each increasing sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of partial evaluation trees, the least upper bound
\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
is a partial evaluation tree as well.

Proof.

Set
\tau=\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
.
We have to show that for every node
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
there is a rule in
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
with conclusion
\tau(\alpha)
and premises the (labels of) the children of
\alpha
in
\tau
.

Recall from
Proposition

4.7
(
2
) that
\tau(\alpha)=\tau_{k_{\alpha}}(\alpha)
, where
k_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}
is the least index
n
where
\tau_{n}(\alpha)
is most defined.
Note that, for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
,
\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)
is finite.
Indeed,
by definition of
\tau
and since the sequence is increasing, we have
\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)=\sup\{\mathsf{br}_{\tau_{n}}(\alpha)\mid n\geq k_{\alpha}\}
, and
\mathsf{br}_{\tau_{n}}(\alpha)
is the number of premises of a rule, for all
n\geq k_{\alpha}
;
all such rules have the same configuration in the conclusion
\mathit{C}(\tau_{n}(\alpha))=\mathit{C}(\tau(\alpha))
, hence,
by condition (
BP
) in
Definition

3.1
, there is
b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{br}_{\tau_{n}}(\alpha)\leq b
, thus
\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)\leq b
.
Then, the set
K=\{k_{\alpha}\}\cup\{k_{\alpha i}\mid i\in 1..\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)\}
is finite and

n=\max K
is finite, hence, as
n\geq k_{\alpha}
and
n\geq k_{\alpha i}
, for all
i\in 1..\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)
, we have

\tau_{n}(\alpha)=\tau(\alpha)
and
\tau_{n}(\alpha i)=\tau(\alpha i)
, for all
i\in 1..\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)
.
Therefore,
{\langle{\tau(\alpha 1)\ldots\tau(\alpha\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)),\tau(\alpha)}\rangle}={\langle{\tau_{n}(\alpha 1)\ldots\tau_{n}(\alpha\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)),\tau_{n}(\alpha)}\rangle}\in\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
, since
\tau_{n}
is a partial evaluation tree.
Thus, by
Definition

4.3
,
\tau
is a partial evaluation tree.
∎

As already mentioned, finite partial evaluation trees model possibly incomplete evaluations.
Then, the relation
\sqsubseteq
models refinement of the evaluation, because if
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
, where
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are finite partial evaluation trees,

\tau^{\prime}
is “more detailed” than
\tau
.
In a sense,
\sqsubseteq
on finite partial evaluation trees abstracts the process of evaluation itself, as we will make precise in the next section.

What about infinite trees?
Similarly to what we have discussed in the introduction,
there are many infinite partial evaluation trees which are difficult to interpret.
For instance, using rules in
Fig.

1
and
Fig.

2
, we can construct the following infinite tree for all
\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}
, where
\Omega=(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})\,(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})
:

\ \displaystyle\frac{\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}}}\ \hskip 6.99997pt\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}}}\ \hskip 6.99997pt\ \displaystyle\frac{\vdots}{{\Omega={(\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})}[{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}\ }{{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{\textsf{?}}}}\

Among all such trees there are some “good” ones, we call them
well-formed
.
Well-formed infinite partial evaluation trees arise as limits of strictly increasing sequences of finite partial evaluation trees,
hence, in a sense, they model the limit of the evaluation process. namely, non-termination.

Definition 4.9.

An infinite partial evaluation tree
\tau
is
well-formed
if,
for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
, if
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))\in\mathit{R}
, then
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
is finite.

In other words, in a well-formed partial evaluation tree all complete subtrees are finite.
The next proposition, together with
Proposition

4.4
, implies that
a well-formed tree
contains a unique infinite path, which is entirely labelled by incomplete judgments.
A similar property on infinite derivations will be enforced by corules in the semantics for divergence in
Section

6
.

Proposition 4.10.

If
\tau
is a well-formed infinite partial evaluation tree then,
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
such that
\left|\alpha\right|=n
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))=\textsf{?}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
.
For
n=0
, we have
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\textsf{?}
, since, otherwise, we would have
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\mathit{r}
, hence,
by
Definition

4.9
,
\tau={\tau}_{\mid_{\varepsilon}}
would be finite, while
\tau
is infinite by hypothesis.

For
n=k+1
, by induction hypothesis, we know there is
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
such that
\left|\alpha\right|=k
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha))=\textsf{?}
.
For all
\beta\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
with
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}h
,
\left|\alpha^{\prime}\right|=k
,
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\alpha
, we have
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\beta))\in\mathit{R}
, because, if
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\beta))=\textsf{?}
, then also
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha^{\prime}))=\textsf{?}
, by
Proposition

4.4
, and, again by
Proposition

4.4
, this implies
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
, which is absurd.
As a consequence, for all such
\beta
, we have that
{\tau}_{\mid_{\beta}}
is finite, as
\tau
is well-formed.

Then, we focus on children of
\alpha
, splitting cases over
\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)
.
If
\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)=0
, then
\alpha
has no children and so
\tau
is finite, which is absurd.
If
h=\mathsf{br}_{\tau}(\alpha)>0
, then, if
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha h))\in\mathit{R}
, since
\tau
is a partial evaluation tree, we get
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha h^{\prime}))\in\mathit{R}
for all
h^{\prime}\leq h
, hence
\tau
is again finite, which is absurd.
Therefore,
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha h))=\textsf{?}
, as needed.
∎

The following result shows that well-formed partial evaluation trees are exactly the least upper bounds of strictly increasing sequences of finite partial evaluation trees.

Proposition 4.11.

The following properties hold:

(1)

for each strictly increasing sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite partial evaluation trees, the least upper bound
\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
is infinite and well-formed;

(2)

for each well-formed infinite partial evaluation tree
\tau
, there is a strictly increasing sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite partial evaluation trees such that
\tau=\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
.

Proof.

To prove
Item

1
, set
\tau=\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
, then, by
Proposition

4.8
, we have that
\tau
is a partial evaluation tree, hence we have only to check that it is infinite and well-formed.

Note that
\tau
is infinite if and only if
\textsf{dom}(\tau)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})
is infinite.
To prove this, it suffices to observe that,
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
h>n
such that
\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})\subset\textsf{dom}(\tau_{h})
, namely, there is
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau_{h})
such that
\alpha\notin\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})
.
This can be proved by induction on the number of
?
in
\tau_{n}
, denoted by
N_{\textsf{?}}(\tau_{n})
, which is finite as
\tau_{n}
is finite.
This follows because,
if
\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})=\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n+1})
, we have
N_{\textsf{?}}(\tau_{n+1})<N_{\textsf{?}}(\tau_{n})
, since
\tau_{n}\sqsubset\tau_{n+1}
implies that there is at least one node
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau_{n})
such that
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau_{n}(\alpha))=\textsf{?}
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau_{n+1}(\alpha))=\mathit{r}
, thus we can apply the induction hypothesis.

To show that
\tau
is well-formed, first recall that, for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
,
we have
\tau(\alpha)=\tau_{n}(\alpha)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, for all
\alpha\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
such that
\tau(\alpha)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, since
\tau_{n}\sqsubseteq\tau
and
\tau_{n}(\alpha)=\tau(\alpha)
, by definition of
\sqsubseteq
, we get
{\tau_{n}}_{\mid_{\alpha}}={\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
;
hence,
{\tau}_{\mid_{\alpha}}
is finite and so
\tau
is well-formed.

To prove
Item

2
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the partial evaluation tree
\tau_{n}
obtained by “cutting”
\tau
at level
n
and defined as follows.
Let
\alpha_{n}\in\textsf{dom}(\tau)
be the node such that
\left|\alpha_{n}\right|=n
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\tau(\alpha_{n}))=\textsf{?}
(which exists by
Proposition

4.10
as
\tau
is well-formed and it is unique thanks to
Proposition

4.4
(
2
)),
then define
\tau_{n}(\beta)=\tau(\beta)
for all
\beta\neq\alpha_{n}\beta^{\prime}
, with
\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}^{+}
, and undefined otherwise.
We have
\tau_{n}\sqsubseteq\tau_{n+1}
, since, by
Proposition

4.4
(
1
),
\alpha_{n+1}=\alpha_{n}i
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.
Finally, by construction, we have
\tau=\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
, as needed.
∎

This important result will be used in the next sections to prove correctness of extended big-step semantics explicitly modelling divergence.

4.2.
The transition relation

As already mentioned, finite partial evaluation trees nicely model intermediate states in the evaluation process of a configuration.
We now make this precise by defining a transition relation
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
between them,
such that, starting from the initial partial evaluation tree
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
, we derive a sequence where, intuitively, at each step we detail the evaluation.
In this way, a sequence ending with a complete tree (a tree containing no
?
) models succesfully terminating computation, whereas an infinite sequence (tending to an infinite partial evaluation tree) models divergence, and a sequence reaching an incomplete tree which cannot further move models a stuck computation.

The one-step transition relation
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
is inductively defined by the rules in
Fig.

3
.
To make the definition clearer, we explicitly annotate the tree with the rule in
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
applied to derive the root of the tree from its children.
In the figure,
\#\rho
denotes the number of premises of
\rho
.
Finally,
\sim_{i}
is the
equality up-to an index
of rules, defined below:

Definition 4.12.

Let
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
and
\rho^{\prime}={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}^{\prime}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}^{\prime}_{m},\,\mathit{c}^{\prime},\,\mathit{r}^{\prime})
be rules in
\mathcal{R}
.
Then, for any index
i\in 1..\min(n,m)
, define
\rho\sim_{i}\rho^{\prime}
if and only if

•

\mathit{c}=\mathit{c}^{\prime}
,

•

for all
k<i
,
\mathit{j}_{k}=\mathit{j}^{\prime}_{k}
, and

•

\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})=\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}^{\prime}_{i})
.

In other words, this equivalence models the fact that rules
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
represent the same computation until the
i
-th configuration included.

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{\scriptsize{(tr-1)}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{ax}_{\textsf{?}}}(\mathit{c}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ {\scriptscriptstyle{(\rho)}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}}\ }\begin{array}[]{l}\#\rho=0\\
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}\\
\mathit{R}(\rho)=\mathit{r}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(tr-2)}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{ax}_{\textsf{?}}}(\mathit{c}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ {\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,1,\textsf{?}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{c}^{\prime}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }\begin{array}[]{l}\#\rho>0\\
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}\\
\mathit{C}(\rho,1)=\mathit{c}^{\prime}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(tr-3)}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\ \ldots\ \tau_{i}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ {\scriptscriptstyle{(\rho^{\prime})}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\ \ldots\ \tau_{i}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}}\ }\begin{array}[]{l}\rho^{\prime}\sim_{i}\rho\\
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},i)=\mathit{r}\\
\#\rho^{\prime}=i\\
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathit{r}^{\prime}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(tr-4)}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\ \ldots\ \tau_{i}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ {\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho^{\prime},i+1,\textsf{?}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\ \ldots\ \tau_{i}\ {\mathit{c}^{\prime}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }\begin{array}[]{l}\rho^{\prime}\sim_{i}\rho\\
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},i)=\mathit{r}\\
\mathit{C}(\rho^{\prime},i+1)=\mathit{c}^{\prime}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(tr-5)}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\ \ldots\ \tau_{i-1}\ \tau_{i}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ {\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\ \ldots\ \tau_{i-1}\ \tau^{\prime}_{i}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }\begin{array}[]{l}\tau_{i}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}_{i}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 3.
Transition relation between partial evaluation trees.

Intuitively, each transition step makes “less incomplete” the partial evaluation tree.
Notably, transition rules apply only to nodes labelled by incomplete judgements (
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
), whereas subtrees whose root is a complete judgement (
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
) cannot move.
In detail:

•

If the last applied rule is
{\mathsf{ax}_{\textsf{?}}}(\mathit{c})
,
we have to find a rule
\rho
with
\mathit{c}
in the conclusion and, if it has no premises we just return
\mathit{R}(\rho)
as result,
otherwise we start evaluating the first premise of such rule.

•

If the last applied rule is
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r})
, then all subtrees are complete, hence, to continue the evaluation, we have to find another rule
\rho^{\prime}
, having, for each
k\in 1..i
, as
k
-th premise the root of
\tau_{k}
.
Then there are two possibilities: if there is an
i+1
-th premise, we start evaluating it, otherwise, we return
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime})
as result.

•

If the last applied rule is a propagation rule
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?})
, then we simply propagate the step made by
\tau_{i}
(the last subtree), which is necessarily incomplete.
After the step,
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
may be complete, hence the last applied rule is
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r}_{\textsf{?}})
.

In
Fig.

4
we report an example of evaluation of a term according to rules in
Fig.

1
, using partial evaluation trees and
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\\[7.74997pt]
\hskip 13.87491pt{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\\[7.74997pt]
\hskip 13.87491pt{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\\[7.74997pt]
\hskip 13.87491pt{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\frac{{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}\hskip 8.04996pt{n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}}{{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{n}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\end{array}

Figure 4.
The evaluation of
(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\,n
using
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
for rules in
Fig.

1
.

As mentioned above, the definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
given in
Fig.

3
nicely models as a transition system an interpreter driven by the big-step rules.
In other words, the one-step transition relation between finite partial evaluation trees specifies an algorithm of incremental evaluation.
5
5

5

Non-determinism can only be caused by intrinsic non-determinism of the big-step semantics, if any.

On the other hand, also the partial order relation
\sqsubseteq
(cf.
Definition

4.6
) models a refinement relation between finite partial evauation trees, even if in a more abstract way.
The next proposition formally proves that these two descriptions agree, namely,
\sqsubseteq
is indeed an abstraction of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

Proposition 4.13.

Let
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
be finite partial evaluation trees, then the following hold:

(1)

if
\tau{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}
then
\tau\sqsubset\tau^{\prime}
, and

(2)

if
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
then
\tau{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Point 1 can be easily proved by induction on the definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.
The proof of point 2 is by induction on
\tau^{\prime}
, denote by
IH
the induction hypothesis.
This is possible as
\tau^{\prime}
is finite by hypothesis.
We can assume
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\textsf{?}
, since in the other case, by
Proposition

4.7
(
1
), we have
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
, hence the thesis is trivial.
We can further assume
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime}))=\textsf{?}
, since, if
\tau^{\prime}=\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{n}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}}\
, then we always have
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{n}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime\prime}
,
because
\tau\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
and we have
\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\prime})=\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
,
\tau^{\prime}(\alpha)=\tau^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\neq\varepsilon
,
\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime}))=\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime\prime}))
and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\textsf{?}
.
Now, if
\tau^{\prime}=\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
(base case), then, since
\textsf{dom}(\tau)\subseteq\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\prime})
and
\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime}))
by definition of
\sqsubseteq
, we have
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
, hence the thesis is trivial.

Let us assume
\tau=\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\tau_{k}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
and
\tau^{\prime}=\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{i}}{{\mathit{c}^{\prime}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
, with, necessarily,
k\leq i
and
\mathit{c}=\mathit{c}^{\prime}
by definition of
\sqsubseteq
.
We have
\tau_{h}\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}_{h}
, for all
h\leq k
, and
by
Proposition

4.4
(
2
), at most
\tau_{k}
is incomplete, that is, for all
h<k
,
\tau_{h}
is complete, namely,
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau_{h}))\in\mathit{R}
, thus, by definition of
\sqsubseteq
, we have
\tau_{h}=\tau^{\prime}_{h}
.
Furthermore, since
\tau_{k}\sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}_{k}
, by
IH
,
we get
\tau_{k}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}_{k}
, hence
\tau{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime\prime}=\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{k}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \sqsubseteq\tau^{\prime}
.

We now show, concluding the proof, by arithmetic induction on
i-k
, that
\tau^{\prime\prime}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}
.
If
i-k=0
, hence
i=k
, we have
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau^{\prime}
, hence the thesis is immediate.
If
i-k>0
, hence
i>k
, setting
\mathit{c}^{\prime\prime}=\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime}_{k+1}))
, by
IH
, we get
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}^{\prime\prime}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}_{k+1}
;
moreover, again by
Proposition

4.4
(
2
), we have
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime}_{k}))\in\mathit{R}
, hence we get

\tau^{\prime\prime}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{k}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{c}^{\prime\prime}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{k}\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\prime}_{k+1}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ =\hat{\tau}

Finally, by arithmetic induction hypothesis, we get
\hat{\tau}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}
, as needed.
∎

We conclude this section by showing that the transition relation
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
agrees with the semantic relation (inductively) defined by
\mathcal{R}
,
namely, the semantic relation captures exactly successful terminating computations in
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

Theorem 4.14.

\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
iff
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau
, where
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
implies
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau
where
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
By definition, if
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
holds, then there is a finite evaluation tree
\tau
in
\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Since
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
by
Definition

4.1
,
\tau
is a (complete) partial evaluation tree as well;
furthermore,
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \sqsubseteq\tau
, hence, by
Proposition

4.13
(
2
), we get the thesis.

\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau
where
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
implies
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Since
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, by
Corollary

4.5
,
\tau
is complete, hence,
it is an evaluation tree in
\mathcal{R}
, thus
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
holds.
∎

5.
Extended big-step semantics: two constructions

In
Section

4
, we have just shown that, given a big-step semantics as in
Definition

3.1
, it is possible to define computations in such semantics, by deriving a transition relation which formally models the evaluation algorithm guided by the rules.
In this way, we are able to distinguish stuck and non-terminating computations as in standard small-step semantics.
This, in a sense, shows that such a distinction is
implicit
in a big-step semantics.

In this section, we aim at showing that we can make such distinction explicit directly by a big-step semantics, without introducing any transition relation modelling single computation steps.
To this end, we describe two constructions that, starting from a big-step semantics, yield extended ones where non-terminating and stuck computations are explicitly distinguished.
These two constructions are in some sense dual to each other, because one explicitly models non-termination, while the other one explicitly models stuckness, and
they are based on well-know ideas: divergence is modelled by
traces
, as suggested by
Leroy and Grall 2009
, while stuckness by an additional special result, as described, for instance, by
Pierce 2002
.
The novel contribution is that, thanks to the general definition of big-step semantics in
Section

3
(cf.
Definition

3.1
), we can provide
general
constructions working on an arbitrary big-step semantics, rather than discussing specific examples, as it is customary in the literature.

In the following, we assume a big-step semantics
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
.

5.1.
Adding traces

The set of
traces
in the big-step semantics is the set
\mathit{C}^{\infty}
of finite and infinite sequences of configurations.
Finite traces are ranged over by
\mathit{t}
, while infinite traces by
\sigma
.

The judgement of trace semantics has shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
, where
\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}\in\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}}=(\mathit{C}^{\star}\times\mathit{R})+\mathit{C}^{\omega}
, that is,

\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}
is either a pair
\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle
of a finite trace and a result, modelling a converging computation, or an infinite trace
\sigma
, modelling divergence.
Intuitively, traces
\mathit{t}
keep track of all the configurations visited during the evaluation, starting from
\mathit{c}
itself.
To define the trace semantics, we construct, starting from
\mathcal{R}
, a new set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
as follows:

Definition 5.1 (Rules for traces).

The set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
consists of the following rules:

finite trace rules:

For each
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
and finite traces
\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{n}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
, define rule
{\mathsf{trace}}({\rho},\,{\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{n}})
as

\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{1})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{1},\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{1})}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{n})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{n},\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{n})}\rangle}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{c}\mathit{t}_{1}\cdots\mathit{t}_{n},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}}\

infinite trace rules:

For each
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
, index
i\in 1..n
, finite traces
\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i-1}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
, and infinite trace
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
, define rule
{\mathsf{trace}_{\infty}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i-1},\,\sigma)
as follows:

\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{1})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{1},\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{1})}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i-1})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{i-1},\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{i-1})}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{c}\mathit{t}_{1}\cdots\mathit{t}_{i-1}\sigma}}\

Finite trace rules enrich big-step rules in
\mathcal{R}
by finite traces, thus modelling computations converging to a final result.
On the other hand, infinite trace rules handle non-termination, modelled by infinite traces:
they propagate divergence, that is, if a configuration in the premises of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
diverges, namely, it evaluates to an infinite trace, then the subsequent premises are ignored and the configuration in the conclusion diverges as well.
Note that all these rules have a non-empty trace in the conclusion, hence only non-empty traces are derivable by such rules.
Finally, observe that the triple
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}}\rangle
is a big-step semantics according to
Definition

3.1
.

The standard inductive interpretation of big-step rules is not enough in this setting:
it can only derive judgements of shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
, because there is no axiom introducing infinite traces, hence they cannot be derived by finite derivations.
In other words, the inductive interpretation of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
can only capture converging computations.
To properly handle divergence, we have to interpret rules
coinductively
, namely, allowing both finite and infinite derivations.
Then, we will write
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
to say that
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
is coinductively derivable by rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
.
It is important to note the following proposition, stating that
enabling infinite derivations does not affect the semantics of converging computations.

Lemma 5.2.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
iff
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
.

Proof.

The right-to-left implication is trivial, because the inductive interpretation is always included in the coinductive one.
The proof of the other direction is by induction on the length of
\mathit{t}
, which is a finite trace.
By hypothesis, we know that
