{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
is derivable by a (possibly infinite) derivation and, by
Definition

5.1
, we know that the last applied rule
\rho^{\mathsf{tr}}
has shape
{\mathsf{trace}}({\rho},\,{\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{n}})
, hence
\mathit{t}=\mathit{c}\mathit{t}_{1}\cdots\mathit{t}_{n}
.
If
\left|\mathit{t}\right|=1
, then
\mathit{t}=\mathit{c}
, and so
n=0
, that is,
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\varepsilon,\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
, because only non-empty traces are derivable, hence
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
holds by
\rho^{\mathsf{tr}}
.
If
\left|\mathit{t}\right|>0
, then, for all
i\in 1..n
,
\left|\mathit{t}_{i}\right|<\left|\mathit{t}\right|
, hence, by induction hypothesis, we get
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\rho,i)}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{i},\mathit{R}(\rho,i)}\rangle}}
, and so
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
holds by
\rho^{\mathsf{tr}}
.
∎

Note that, following the same inductive strategy as the above proof,
we can prove that actually a derivation for a judgement of shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
is necessarily finite.
This is essentially due to the fact that rules are
productive
, meaning that the trace in the conclusion is always strictly larger than those in the premises.

We show in
Fig.

5
the rules obtained by applying
Definition

5.1
, starting from meta-rule
(app)
of the example in
Fig.

1
(for the other meta-rules the outcome is analogous).

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(app-tr)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{1},\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{2},\mathit{v}_{2}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{v}}\rangle}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{(\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2})\mathit{t}_{1}\mathit{t}_{2}\mathit{t},\mathit{v}}\rangle}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[12.91663pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(div-app-1)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{(\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2})\sigma}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 6.99997pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(div-app-2)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{1},\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{(\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2})\mathit{t}_{1}\sigma}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[12.91663pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(div-app-3)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{1},\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{2},\mathit{v}_{2}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.99997pt{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{(\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2})\mathit{t}_{1}\mathit{t}_{2}\sigma}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 5.
Trace semantics for application

For instance, set
\omega=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}
, hence
\Omega=\omega\,\omega
(cf. page
4.1
), and
\sigma_{\Omega}
the infinite trace
\Omega\omega\omega\Omega\omega\omega\ldots
,
it is easy to see that the judgment
{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{t}_{\Omega}}
can be derived by the following infinite derivation:
6
6

6

To help the reader, we add equivalent expressions with a grey background.

\ \displaystyle\frac{\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\omega}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\omega,\omega}\rangle}}}\ \hskip 6.99997pt\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\omega}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\omega,\omega}\rangle}}}\ \hskip 6.99997pt\ \displaystyle\frac{\vdots}{{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\Omega=$}{(\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})}[{\omega}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma_{\Omega}}}\ }{{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\Omega\omega\omega\sigma_{\Omega}\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$=\sigma_{\Omega}$}}}\

Note that
only
the judgment
{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma_{\Omega}}
can be derived, that is, the trace semantics of
\Omega
is uniquely determined to be
\sigma_{\Omega}
,
since the infinite derivation forces the equation
\sigma_{\Omega}=\Omega\omega\omega\sigma_{\Omega}
.

To check that the construction in
Definition

5.1
is a correct extension of the given big-step semantics,
we have to show it is
conservative
, in the sense that it does not affect the semantics of converging computations, as formally stated below.

Theorem 5.3.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
for some
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
iff
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.2
, we know that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
iff
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
.
Then, the thesis follows by proving
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
, for some
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
, iff
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
,
by a straightforward induction on rules.
∎

We conclude this subsection by showing a coinductive proof principle associated with trace semantics, which allows us to prove that a predicate on configurations ensures the existence of a non-terminating computation.

Lemma 5.4.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathit{C}
be a set.
If, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, there are
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})\in\mathcal{R}
and
i\in 1..n

such that

(1)

for all
k<i
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, and

(2)

\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}

then, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, there exists
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
such that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
.

Proof.

First of all, for each
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, we construct a trace
\sigma_{\mathit{c}}\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
, which will be the candidate trace to prove the thesis.
By hypothesis (
Item

1
), there is a rule
\rho_{\mathit{c}}={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}^{\mathit{c}}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n_{\mathit{c}}}^{\mathit{c}},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r}_{\mathit{c}})
and an index
i_{\mathit{c}}\in 1..n_{\mathit{c}}
such that, for all
k<i_{\mathit{c}}
, we have
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}^{\mathit{c}}
.
Therefore, by
Theorem

5.3
, there are finite traces
\mathit{t}_{1}^{\mathit{c}},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i_{\mathit{c}}-1}^{\mathit{c}}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
such that for all
k<i_{\mathit{c}}
we have
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k}^{\mathit{c}})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{k}^{\mathit{c}},\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{k}^{\mathit{c}})}\rangle}}
,
and, in addition (
Item

2
), we know that
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i_{\mathit{c}}}^{\mathit{c}})\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then, for each
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, we can introduce a variable
X_{\mathit{c}}
and define an equation
X_{\mathit{c}}=\mathit{c}\cdot\mathit{t}_{1}^{\mathit{c}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\mathit{t}_{i_{\mathit{c}}-1}^{\mathit{c}}\cdot X_{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i_{\mathit{c}}}^{\mathit{c}})}
.
The set of all such equations is a guarded system of equations, which thus has a unique solution, namely, a function
{s}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathit{C}^{\omega}}
such that, for each
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
we have
s(\mathit{c})=\mathit{c}\cdot\mathit{t}_{1}^{\mathit{c}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\mathit{t}_{i_{\mathit{c}}-1}^{\mathit{c}}\cdot s(\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i_{\mathit{c}}}^{\mathit{c}}))
.
7
7

7

This argument can be made more precise using coalgebras
(
Rutten 2000
)
, in particular the fact that
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathit{C}^{\omega}
carry, respectively, a coalgebra and a corecursive algebra
(
Capretta
et al. 2009
)
structure for the functor
X\mapsto\mathit{C}^{\star}\times X
.

We now have to prove that, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{s(\mathit{c})}
.
To this end,
consider the set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{{\langle{\mathit{c},s(\mathit{c})}\rangle}\mid\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{{\langle{\mathit{c},{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}\rangle}\mid\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}\}
,
then the proof is by coinduction.
Let
{\langle{\mathit{c},\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then we have to find a rule
{\langle{\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
such that, for all
k\in 1..n
,
{\langle{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k}),\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\rangle}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
We have two cases:

•

if
\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}=s(\mathit{c})
, then the needed rule is
{\mathsf{trace}_{\infty}}(\rho_{\mathit{c}},\,i_{\mathit{c}},\,\mathit{t}_{1}^{\mathit{c}},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i_{\mathit{c}}-1}^{\mathit{c}},\,s(\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i_{\mathit{c}}}^{\mathit{c}})))
, and

•

if
\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}={\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}
, then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
, by construction of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, hence
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
is the conclusion of a finite trace rule, where all premises are still derivable, thus in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
by construction.

∎

5.2.
Adding
wrong

A well-known technique
(
Abadi and
Cardelli 1996
;
Pierce 2002
)
to distinguish between stuck and diverging computations, in a sense “dual” to the previous one, is to add a special result
wrong
, so that
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
means that the evaluation of
\mathit{c}
goes stuck.

In this case, defining a general and “automatic” version of the construction, starting from an arbitrary big-step semantics
{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}
, is a non-trivial problem.
Our solution is based on the equivalence on rules defined in
Definition

4.12
(equality up to an index), which allows us to define when
wrong
can be introduced.

The extended judgement has shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{wr}}}
where
\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{wr}}\in\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}=\mathit{R}+\{\textsf{wrong}\}
, that is, it is either a result or an error.
To define the extended semantics, we construct, starting from
\mathcal{R}
, an extended set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
as follows:

Definition 5.5 (Rules for
wrong
).

The set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
is obtained by adding to
\mathcal{R}
the following rules:

wrong configuration rules:

For each configuration
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}
such that there is no rule
\rho
in
\mathcal{R}
with
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, define rule
{\textsf{wrong}}(\mathit{c})
as
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\
.

wrong result rules:

For each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
, index
i\in 1..n
, and result
\mathit{r}^{\prime}\in\mathit{R}
,
if, for all rules
\rho^{\prime}
such that
\rho\sim_{i}\rho^{\prime}
,
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},i)\neq\mathit{r}^{\prime}
, then define rule
{\textsf{wrong}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{r}^{\prime})
as

\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{j}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathit{j}_{i-1}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\

wrong
propagation rules:

These rules propagate
wrong
analogously to those for divergence propagation:
For each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
and index
i\in 1..n
, define rule
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\textsf{wrong})
as

\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{j}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathit{j}_{i-1}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\

Wrong configurations rules simply say that, if there is no rule for a given configuration, then we can derive
wrong
.
Wrong result rules, instead,
derive
wrong
whenever the configuration in a premise of a rule reduces to a result which is not admitted in such (and any equivalent) rule.
We also call these two kinds of rules
wrong
introduction rules, as they introduce
wrong
in the conclusion without having it in the premises.
Finally,
wrong
propagation rules say that, if a configuration in a premise of some rule in
\mathcal{R}
goes wrong, then
the subsequent premises are ignored and the configuration in the conclusion goes wrong as well.
Note that
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{wr}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}}\rangle
is a big-step semantics according to
Definition

3.1
.

In this case, the standard inductive interpretation is enough to get the correct semantics, because, intuitively, an error, if any, occurs after a finite number of steps.
Then, we write
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{wr}}}
when the judgment
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{wr}}}
is inductively derivable by rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
.

We show in
Fig.

6
the meta-rules for
wrong
introduction and propagation constructed starting from those for application and successor in
Fig.

1
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(wrong-app)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{n}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(wrong-succ)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}}{{\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(prop-app-1)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(prop-app-2)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(prop-app-3)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\hskip 6.47491pt{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(prop-succ)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}{{\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 6.
Semantics with
wrong
for application and successor

For instance, rule
(wrong-app)
is introduced since in the original semantics there is rule
(app)
with
\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}
in the conclusion and
\mathit{e}_{1}
in the first premise, but
there is no equivalent rule (that is, with
\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}
in the conclusion and
\mathit{e}_{1}
in the first premise) such that the result in the first premise is
\mathit{n}
.
Intuitively, this means that
\mathit{n}
is a wrong result for the evaluation of the first argument of an application.

Like the previous construction, the
wrong
construction is a correct extension of
\mathcal{R}
, namely, it is conservative.

Theorem 5.6.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
iff
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.

Proof.

The right-to-left implication is trivial, as
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
by
Definition

5.5
.
The proof of the other direction is by induction on rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
.
The only relevant cases are rules in
\mathcal{R}
, because rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\setminus\mathcal{R}
allow only the derivation of judgements of shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.
Hence, the thesis is immediate.
∎

5.3.
Correctness of constructions

We now prove correctness of the trace and
wrong
constructions, by showing they capture diverging and stuck computations, respectively, as defined by the transition relation
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
introduced in
Section

4.2
.
This provides us a coherence result for our approach.

First of all, note that both constructions correctly capture converging computations, because, if restricted to such computations, by
Theorems

5.3
and
5.6
, the constructions are both equivalent to the original big-step semantics.
Hence, in the following, we focus only on diverging and stuck computations, respectively.

Correctness of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}

Given a partial evaluation tree
\tau
, we write
\tau{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
meaning that there is an infinite sequence of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
-steps starting from
\tau
.
Then, the theorem we want to prove is the following:

Theorem 5.7.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
, for some
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
,
iff
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

To prove this result, we need to relate evaluation trees (a.k.a. derivations) in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
(cf.
Definition

5.1
) to partial evaluation trees in
\mathcal{R}
(cf.
Definition

4.3
).
To this end, we define a function
{u_{\textsf{?}}}:{\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}}}\rightarrow{\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}}
, which essentially forgets traces, as follows:

u_{\textsf{?}}({\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle})=\mathit{r}
and
u_{\textsf{?}}(\sigma)=\textsf{?}
.
We can extend this function to judgements, mapping
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
to
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{u_{\textsf{?}}(\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}})}
, and to rules, mapping

{\mathsf{trace}}({\rho},\,{\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{n}})
to
\rho
and
{\mathsf{trace}_{\infty}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i-1},\,\sigma)
to
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?})
.
Finally, we get a function

\mathsf{erase}
that transforms an evaluation tree
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
into a partial evaluation tree, defined by
\mathsf{erase}(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}})=u_{\textsf{?}}\circ\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
,
that is, relying on the fact that a tree is a (partial) function, we postcompose
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
with
u_{\textsf{?}}
;
in other words,
this means that we apply
u_{\textsf{?}}
to all judgements labeling a node in
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
, thus erasing traces.
Since
u_{\textsf{?}}
transforms rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
into rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{?}}
,
\mathsf{erase}(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}})
is indeed a partial evaluation tree and the following equalities between trees hold:

\begin{split}\mathsf{erase}\left({\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{trace}}({\rho},\,{\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{n}}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{n}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}}\ }\right)&={\scriptscriptstyle{(\rho)}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{1}\right)\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{n}\right)}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}}\ }\\[8.61108pt]
\mathsf{erase}\left({\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{trace}_{\infty}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i-1},\,\sigma))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma^{\prime}}}\ }\right)&={\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?}))}\!\!\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{1}\right)\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}\right)}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ }\end{split}

Note that, by construction,
\textsf{dom}(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}})=\textsf{dom}(\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right))
, hence,

\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
is finite iff
\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right)
is finite and

\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
is infinite iff
\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right)
is infinite.
Furthermore, since, as we have already observed,
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
is finite iff
\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}})={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
, we have that

\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right)
is complete iff
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
is finite and

\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right)
is well-formed iff
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
is infinite (cf.
Definition

4.9
).

Lemma 5.8.

If
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
holds by an infinite evaluation tree
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
, then there is a sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\tau_{n}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau_{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau_{0}=\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
, and
\bigsqcup\tau_{n}=\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right)
.

Proof.

Since
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
is infinite,
\mathsf{erase}\left(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}\right)=\tau
is a well-formed infinite partial evaluation treee and, by
Proposition

4.11
(
2
), there is a strictly increasing sequence
(\tau^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite partial evaluation trees such that
\bigsqcup\tau^{\prime}_{n}=\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}_{0}=\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
.
By
Proposition

4.13
(
2
), since for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\tau^{\prime}_{n}\sqsubset\tau^{\prime}_{n+1}
, we get
\tau^{\prime}_{n}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}_{n+1}
, and, since
\tau^{\prime}_{n}\neq\tau^{\prime}_{n+1}
, this sequence of steps is not empty.
Hence, we can construct a sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\tau_{0}=\tau^{\prime}_{0}=\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
,
\tau_{n}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau_{n+1}
and
\bigsqcup\tau_{n}=\tau
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 5.9.

Let
\tau
be a well-formed infinite partial evaluation tree with
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
.
Then,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
holds for some
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
.

Proof.

The thesis follows from
Lemma

5.4
, applied to the set
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathit{C}
defined as follows:

\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
iff
\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\mathit{c}
, for some infinite well-formed partial evaluation tree
\tau
.
Let
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, then
\mathit{c}=\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))
and the last applied rule in
\tau
is
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?})
, for some
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
.
Then, we have
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, for all
k<i
and
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})=\mathit{C}(\mathsf{r}({\tau}_{\mid_{i}}))
and
{\tau}_{\mid_{i}}
is an infinite well-formed partial evaluation tree.
Therefore,
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}
, and so the hypotheses of
Lemma

5.4
are satisfied.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.7
.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
for some
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
implies
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.
Since
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
holds and
\sigma
is infinite, by (a consequence of)
Lemma

5.2
, there is an infinite evaluation tree
\tau^{\mathsf{tr}}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}
such that
\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\mathsf{tr}})={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
.
Then, by
Lemma

5.8
we get the thesis.

\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
implies
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
for some
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
.
By definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
, there is an infinite sequence
(\tau_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\tau_{0}=\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
and, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau_{n}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau_{n+1}
, hence, by
Proposition

4.13
(
1
), we get
\tau_{n}\sqsubset\tau_{n+1}
.
By
Proposition

4.11
(
1
), we have that
\tau=\bigsqcup\tau_{n}
is a well-formed infinite partial evaluation tree, hence we get the thesis by
Lemma

5.9
.
∎

Correctness of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}

We now show that the construction in
Section

5.2
correctly models stuck computation in
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

The proof relies on the following lemma.
We say that a (finite) partial evaluation tree
\tau
is
irreducible
if there is no
\tau^{\prime}
such that
\tau{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau^{\prime}
, and it is
stuck
if it is irreducible and
\mathit{R}_{\textsf{?}}(\mathsf{r}(\tau))=\textsf{?}
.
Note that, by
Proposition

4.7
(
1
) and
Proposition

4.13
(
1
), a complete partial evaluation tree
\tau
is irreducible.

Lemma 5.10.

If
\tau
is a stuck partial evaluation tree with
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
, then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
holds.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\tau
, splitting cases on the last applied rule.

Case:
{\mathsf{ax}_{\textsf{?}}}(\mathit{c})

Since
\tau
is stuck, by definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(cf.
Fig.

3
first and second clauses), there is no rule
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, hence
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
holds, by applying
{\textsf{wrong}}(\mathit{c})
.

Case:
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r})

Suppose
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r}^{\prime})
and
i\in 1..n
, by hypothesis, for all
k<i
,
{\tau}_{\mid_{k}}
is a complete partial evaluation tree of
\mathit{j}_{k}
, hence we know that
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
holds.
Since
\tau
is stuck, by definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(cf.
Fig.

3
third and fourth clauses), there is no rule
\rho^{\prime}\sim_{i}\rho
with
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},i)=\mathit{r}
, hence
{\textsf{wrong}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{r})\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
.
By
Theorem

5.6
we get
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, for all
k<i
, hence
applying
{\textsf{wrong}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{r})
, we get
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.

Case:
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?})

Suppose
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r}^{\prime})
and
i\in 1..n
, by hypothesis, for all
k<i
,
{\tau}_{\mid_{k}}
is a complete partial evaluation tree of
\mathit{j}_{k}
, hence we know that
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
holds.
Set
\mathit{c}_{i}=\mathit{C}(\rho,i)
, then, since
\tau
is stuck, by definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(cf.
Fig.

3
clause
(tr-5)
), the subtree
{\tau}_{\mid_{i}}
is stuck as well and
\mathsf{r}({\tau}_{\mid_{i}})={\mathit{c}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
.
By
Theorem

5.6
, we get
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, for all
k<i
, and,
by induction hypothesis, we get
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
, hence, applying rule
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\textsf{wrong})
, we get
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.
∎

Lemma 5.11.

If
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
, then
there is a stuck partial evaluation tree
\tau
with
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
.
It is enough to consider only rules with
wrong
in the conclusion, hence we have the following three cases:

Case:
{\textsf{wrong}}(\mathit{c})

By
Definition

5.5
, there is no rule
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, thus
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
is stuck.

Case:
{\textsf{wrong}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{r})

By
Definition

5.5
, assuming
\rho\equiv{\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r}^{\prime})
, there is no rule
\rho^{\prime}\sim_{i}\rho
such that
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},i)=\mathit{r}
;
then, by
Theorem

5.6
, for all
k\leq i
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
holds, hence there is a finite and complete partial evaluation tree
\tau_{k}
with
\mathsf{r}(\tau_{k})=\mathit{j}_{k}
.
Therefore, applying rule
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\mathit{r})
to
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{i}
, we get a partial evaluation tree, which is stuck, by definition of
{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

Case:
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\textsf{wrong})

Suppose
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
and
\mathit{c}_{i}=\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})
, then, by induction hypothesis, we get that there is a stuck tree
\tau^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{r}(\tau^{\prime})={\mathit{c}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
;
then, by
Theorem

5.6
, for all
k<i
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
holds, hence there is a finite and complete partial evaluation tree
\tau_{k}
with
\mathsf{r}(\tau_{k})=\mathit{j}_{k}
.
Therefore, applying
{\mathsf{pev}_{\textsf{?}}}(\rho,i,\textsf{?})
to
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{i-1},\tau^{\prime}
, we get a stuck tree.
∎

Theorem 5.12.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}

iff
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau
, where
\tau
is stuck.

Proof.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
implies
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau
where
\tau
is stuck.
By
Lemma

5.11
we get a stuck partial evaluation tree
\tau
with
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
,
hence the thesis follows by
Proposition

4.13
(
2
), as we trivially have
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ \sqsubseteq\tau
.

\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\star}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\tau
where
\tau
is stuck implies
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.
It follows immediately from
Lemma

5.10
, since
\mathsf{r}(\tau)={\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}
by hypothesis.
∎

6.
Divergence by coaxioms

As we have described in
Section

5.1
, traces allow us to explicitly model divergence, provided that we interpret rules coinductively: a configuration diverges if it evaluates to an infinite trace.
However, the resulting semantics is somewhat redundant: traces keep track of all configurations visited during the evaluation, while we are just interested in whether there is a final result or non-termination, and a configuration may evaluate to many different infinite traces, hence divergence is modelled in many ways.
In this section we show how coaxioms (cf.
Definition

2.1
in
Section

2
) can be succesfully adopted to achieve a more abstract model of divergence, removing this redundancy.
Basically, we present a systematic definition of the approach discussed by
Ancona
et al. 2017b
.

The key idea is to regard divergence just as a special result
\infty
, that, like infinite traces (cf.
Definition

5.1
) and
wrong
(cf.
Section

5.2
), can only be propagated by big-step rules.
To this end, we define yet another construction, extending a given big-step semantics.

Let us assume a big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
.
Then, the extended judgement has shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
where
\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}_{\infty}=\mathit{R}+\{\infty\}
, that is, it is either a result or divergence.
To define the extended semantics, we construct, starting from
\mathcal{R}
, a new set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
as follows:

Definition 6.1 (Rules for divergence).

The set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
is obtained by adding to
\mathcal{R}
the following rules:

divergence propagation rules:

For each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
and index
i\in 1..n
, define rule
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\infty)
as

\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{j}_{1}\hskip 6.99997pt\ldots\hskip 6.99997pt\mathit{j}_{i-1}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}\

These additional rules
propagate divergence, that is, if a configuration in the premises of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
diverges, then the subsequent premises are ignored and the configuration in the conclusion diverges as well.
This is very similar to infinite trace rules, but here we do not need to construct traces to represent divergence.
Note that the triple
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\infty}}\rangle
is a big-step semantics according to
Definition

3.1
.

Now the question is: how do we interpret such rules?
The standard inductive interpretation of big-step rules, as for trace semantics, is not enough in this setting, since there is no axiom introducing
\infty
,
hence it cannot be derived by finite derivations.
In other words, the inductive interpretation of
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
can only capture converging computations, hence it is equivalent to the inductive interpretation of
\mathcal{R}
.
On the other hand, differently from trace semantics, even the coinductive interpretation cannot provide the expected semantics: it allows the derivation of too many judgements.
For instance, in
Fig.

7
, we report the divergence propagation rules obtained starting from meta-rule
(app)
of the example in
Fig.

1
(for other meta-rules the outcome is analogous);
then, using these rules (and the original ones in
Fig.

1
), we can build the following infinite derivation for
\Omega
, which is correct for any
\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}_{\infty}
.

\ \displaystyle\frac{\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\omega}}\ \hskip 6.99997pt\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\omega}}\ \hskip 6.99997pt\ \displaystyle\frac{\vdots}{{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\Omega=$}{(\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})}[{\omega}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}}\ }{{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}}\

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(div-app-1)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 15.00002pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(div-app-2)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[12.91663pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(div-app-3)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\hskip 6.99997pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\hskip 6.99997pt{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}{{\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 7.
Divergence propagation rules for application

Intuitively, we would like to allow infinite derivations only to derive divergence, namely, judgments of shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.
Inference systems with corules are precisely the tool enabling this kind of refinement.
That is, in addition to divergence propagation rules, we can add appropriate corules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}
for divergence, as defined below.

Definition 6.2 (Coaxioms for divergence).

The set of corules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}
consists of the following coaxioms:

coaxioms for divergence:

for each configuration
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}
, define coaxiom
{\mathsf{div}_{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathit{c})
as
\genfrac{}{}{1.5pt}{0}{}{\eval{\conf}{\divres}}
.

As described in
Section

2
, coaxioms impose additional conditions on infinite derivations to be considered correct:
a judgement
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
is derivable in
\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle
iff it has an arbitrary (finite or infinite) derivation in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
, whose nodes all have a finite derivation in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}
, that is, using both rules and corules.
We will write
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
when
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
is derivable in
\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle
.

In the above example,
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
holds iff
\mathit{r}_{\infty}=\infty
, because
{\Omega}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
has no finite derivation in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}
, for any
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
.
In the case of the trace construction (cf.
Section

5.1
), coaxioms are not needed as rules are
productive
, because the trace in the conclusion is always strictly larger than those in the premises, see
Definition

5.1
.

To check that the construction in
Definition

6.1
and
Definition

6.2
is a correct extension of the given big-step semantics,
as for trace semantics, we have to show it is conservative, in the sense that it does not affect the semantics of converging computations, as formally stated below.

Theorem 6.3.

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
iff
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.

Proof.

The right-to-left implication is trivial as
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
by
Definition

6.1
.
To get the other direction, note that if
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
then we have
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Hence, we prove by induction on rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}
that, if
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
then
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
The cases of coaxiom
{\mathsf{div}_{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathit{c})
and divergence propagation
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\infty)
are both empty, as the conclusion of such rules has shape
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.
The only relevant case is that of a rule
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
, for which the thesis follows immediately.
∎

Inference systems with corules come with the bounded coinduction principle (cf.
Theorem

2.2
).
Thanks to such principle, we can define a coinductive proof principle,
which allows us to prove that a predicate on configurations ensures the existence of a non-terminating computation.

Lemma 6.4.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathit{C}
be a set.
If, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, there are
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
and
i\in 1..n

such that

(1)

for all
k<i
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, and

(2)

\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}

then, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
,
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.

Proof.

Consider the set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{{\langle{\mathit{c},\infty}\rangle}\mid\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{{\langle{\mathit{c},\mathit{r}}\rangle}\mid\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}\}
,
then the proof is by bounded coinduction (cf.
Theorem

2.2
).

Boundedness:

We have to show that, for all
{\langle{\mathit{c},\mathit{r}_{\infty}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
holds.
This is easy because, if
\mathit{r}_{\infty}=\infty
, then this holds by coaxiom
{\mathsf{div}_{\mathsf{co}}}(\mathit{c})
, otherwise
\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}
and
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
, hence this holds since
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}
.

Consistency:

We have to show that, for all
{\langle{\mathit{c},\mathit{r}_{\infty}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, there is a rule
{\langle{\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
such that, for all
k\in 1..n
,
{\langle{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k}),\mathit{R}_{\infty}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\rangle}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

There are two cases:

•:

If
\mathit{r}_{\infty}=\infty
, then by hypothesis (
Item

1
), we have a rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})\in\mathcal{R}
and an index
i\in 1..n
such that, for all
k<i
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
and
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then, the needed rule is
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\infty)
.

•:

If
\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}
, then, by construction of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
, hence, there is a rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r}_{\infty})\in\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
, where, for all
k\in 1..n
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
holds, and so
{\langle{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k}),\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\rangle}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

∎

The reader may have noticed that most definitions and results in this section are very similar to those provided for trace semantics in
Section

5.1
.
This is not a coincidence, indeed, we now formally prove this semantics is an
abstraction
of trace semantics.

Intuitively, if we are only interested in modelling convergence or divergence, traces are useless, in the sense that it is only relevant to know whether the trace is infinite or not and, in case it is finite, the final result.
We can model this intuition by a (surjective) function
{u}:{\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}}}\rightarrow{\mathit{R}_{\infty}}
simply forgetting traces, that is,

u({\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle})=\mathit{r}
and
u(\sigma)=\infty
, with
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
and
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
.

Then, we aim at proving the following result:

Theorem 6.5.

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
iff
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}
, for some
\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}
such that
\mathit{r}_{\infty}=u(\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}})
.

In a diagrammatic form,
Theorem

6.5
says that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\wp(\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{!}}
\textstyle{\wp(\mathit{R}_{\infty})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathit{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\mathsf{tr}}}
\scriptstyle{\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\infty}}

where
{u_{!}}:{\wp(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}})}\rightarrow{\wp(\mathcal{R}_{\infty})}
is the direct image of
u
,

{\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\mathsf{tr}}}:{\mathit{C}}\rightarrow{\wp(\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}})}
is defined by
\llbracket\mathit{c}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{tr}}=\{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}\in\mathit{Tr}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{R}}\mid\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\mathit{r}_{\mathsf{tr}}}\}
, and

{\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\infty}}:{\mathit{C}}\rightarrow{\wp(\mathit{R}_{\infty})}
is defined by
\llbracket\mathit{c}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{tr}}=\{\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}_{\infty}\mid{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}\}
.

Proof.

The statement can be split in the following two points:

(1)

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
iff
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
, for some
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{C}^{\star}
, and

(2)

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
iff
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
, for some
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
.

The first point follows immediately from
Theorem

5.3
and
Theorem

6.3
, as
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t},\mathit{r}}\rangle}}
are both equivalent to
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Then, we have only to prove the second point.

The left-to-right implication follows applying
Lemma

5.4
to the set
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}=\{\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}\mid{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}\}
.
If
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, then
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
is derived by a rule
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\infty)
for some
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
and
i\in 1..n
,
hence we have
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, which implies
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
by
Theorem

6.3
, for all
k<i
, and

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
, that is,
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, because these judgements are the premises of
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\infty)
.
Therefore, the hypotheses of
Lemma

5.4
are satisfied and we get, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma_{\mathit{c}}}
, for some
\sigma_{\mathit{c}}\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
, hence
u(\sigma_{\mathit{c}})=\infty
.

Similarly, the right-to-left implication follows applying
Lemma

6.4
to the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{tr}}=\{\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}\mid\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}\mbox{ for some }\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}\}
.
If
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{tr}}
, then, for some
\sigma\in\mathit{C}^{\omega}
,
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma}
is derived by a rule
{\mathsf{trace}_{\infty}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i-1},\,\sigma^{\prime})
, for some
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
and
i\in 1..n
,
hence we have
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{{\langle{\mathit{t}_{k},\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\rangle}}
,
which implies
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
by
Theorem

5.3
, for all
k<i
, and

\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tr}}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{tr}}}\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
, that is,
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{tr}}
, because these judgements are the premises of the rule
{\mathsf{trace}_{\infty}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{t}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{t}_{i-1},\,\sigma^{\prime})
.
Therefore, the hypotheses of
Lemma

6.4
are satisfied and we get, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{tr}}
,
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of
Theorem

6.5
and
Theorem

5.7
, we get the following corollary, stating that the construction given by
Definitions

6.1
and
6.2
correctly models diverging computations:

Corollary 6.6.

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}

iff
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\ {\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.69046pt\ \ \hskip 5.69046pt}\negthickspace{}^{\omega}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
.

Total semantics

We now briefly describe how we can combine the presented constructions in order to get a semantics modelling
all
computations as defined in
Section

4.2
.
In particular, we will use the
wrong
construction to model stuck computations and the construction in this section to model divergence, because they are more similar to each other.

Let us consider a big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
.
We add to
\mathit{R}
two special values to model stuckness and divergence, defining
\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}=\mathit{R}+\{\textsf{wrong}\}+\{\infty\}
.
Then, we have to add appropriate rules to handle these two special results: the idea is to add “simultanously” rules from
Definition

5.5
and from
Definition

6.1
, that is,
we define
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\cup\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
.
Note that, since both
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
extend
\mathcal{R}
, we have
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}
.
In addition, the triple
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}}\rangle
is a big-step semantics according to
Definition

3.1
.
Finally, to properly model divergence, we have to add corules from
Definition

6.2
, so that infinite derivations are only allowed to prove divergence.

Since, as we have noticed, all the presented constructions yield a big-step semantics, starting from another one, we can also try to combine them “sequentially”.
Of course, there are two possibilities: either we first apply the
wrong
construction or the divergence construction.
Nicely, it is not difficult to check that all these possibilities yield the same big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}}\rangle
, as depicted below:

\textstyle{{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{wr}}
\scriptstyle{\infty}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{tot}}
\textstyle{{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{wr}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\infty}
\textstyle{{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\infty}}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{wr}}
\textstyle{{\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}}\rangle}}

Thanks to the commutativity of the above diagram, we can exploit results proved for the various constructions to get properties of this last construction, as stated below.

Proposition 6.7.

The following facts hold:

(1)

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
iff
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
,

(2)

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
iff
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
,

(3)

{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
iff
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.

Proof.

All right-to-left implication are trivial, as
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}},\mathcal{R}_{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}}
.
The other implications follow from
Theorems

5.6
and
6.3
, relying on the above commutative diagram.
∎

Corollary 6.8.

For any configuration
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}
, one of the following holds:

•

either
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, for some
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
,

•

or
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
,

•

or
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{tot}},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward from
Proposition

6.7
and
Theorems

6.5
,
5.12
and
5.7
, since the partial evaluation tree
\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{?}}}\
, either converges to a tree, which is either complete or stuck, or diverges.
∎

Note that these three possibilities in general are not mutually exclusive, that is, for instance, a configuration can both converge to a result and diverge.
This is due to the fact that big-step rules can define a non-deterministic behaviour.

7.
Expressing and proving soundness

A predicate (for instance, a typing judgment) is
sound
when, informally, a program satisfying such predicate (
e.g.
, a well-typed program) cannot
go wrong
, following Robin Milner’s slogan
(
Milner 1978
)
.
In small-step style, as firstly formulated by
Wright and
Felleisen 1994
, this is naturally expressed as follows: well-typed programs never reduce to terms which neither are values, nor can be further reduced (called
stuck
terms).
The standard technique to ensure soundness is by subject reduction (well-typedness is preserved by reduction) and progress (a well-typed term is not stuck).

In standard (inductive) big-step semantics, soundness, as described above, cannot even be expressed, because diverging and stuck computations are not distinguishable.

Constructions presented in the previous sections make this distinction explicit, hence they allow us to reason about soundness with respect to a big-step semantics.
In this section, we discuss how soundness can be expressed and we will provide sufficient conditions.
In other words, we provide a proof technique to show the soundness of a predicate with respect to a big-step semantics.

It is important to highlight the following about the presented approach to soundness.
First, even though type systems are the paradigmatic example, we will consider a generic predicate on configurations, hence our approach could be instantiated with other kinds of predicates.
Second, depending on the kind of construction considered, we can express different flavours of soundness, which will have different proof techniques.
Finally, and more importantly,
as mentioned in the introduction, the extended semantics is only needed
to prove the correctness of the technique, whereas to
apply
the technique for a given big-step semantics it is enough to reason on the original rules.

7.1.
Expressing soundness

In the following, we assume a big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
, and
an
indexed predicate on configurations and results
, that is,
a family
\Pi={\langle{\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota},\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}}\rangle}_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}
, for
\mathit{I}
set of
indexes
, with
\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}\subseteq\mathit{C}
and
\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}\subseteq\mathit{R}
.
A representative case is that, as in the examples of
Section

8
, predicates on configurations and results are typing judgments and the indexes are types;
however, this setting is more general and so the proof technique could be applied to other kinds of predicates.
When there is no ambiguity, we also denote by
\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
and
\Pi^{\mathit{R}}
, respectively, the corresponding predicates
\bigcup_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}
and
\bigcup_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}
on
\mathit{C}
and
\mathit{R}
(
e.g.
, to be well-typed with an arbitrary type).

To discuss how to express soundness of
\Pi
, first of all note that, in the non-deterministic case (that is, there is possibly more than one computation for a configuration), we can distinguish two flavours of soundness, see,
e.g.
,
(
De Nicola and
Hennessy 1984
)
:

soundness-must:

(or simply soundness) no computation can be stuck

soundness-may:

at least one computation is not stuck

Soundness-must is the standard soundness in small-step semantics, and can be expressed by the
wrong
construction
as follows:

soundness-must:

If
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}

Soundness-must
cannot
be expressed by the constructions making divergence explicit,
because stuck computations are not explicitly modelled.
In contrast, soundness-may can be expressed, for instance, by the divergence construction as follows:

soundness-may:

If
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, then
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
, for some
\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}_{\infty}

whereas it cannot be expressed by the
wrong
construction, since diverging computations are not modelled.
Note that, instead, using the total semantics, we can express both flavours of soundness, as it models both diverging and stuck computations.

Of course soundness-must and soundness-may coincide in the deterministic case.
Finally, note that indexes (
e.g.
, the specific types of configurations and results) do not play any role in the above statements.
However, they are relevant in the notion of
strong soundness
, introduced by
Wright and
Felleisen 1994
.
Strong soundness holds (in must or may flavour) if soundness holds (in must or may flavour), and, moreover, configurations satisfying
\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}
(
e.g.
, having a given type) produce results, if any, satisfying
\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}
(
e.g.
, of the same type).
Note that soundness alone does not even guarantee to obtain a result satisfying
\Pi^{\mathit{R}}
(
e.g.
, a well-typed result).
The sufficient conditions introduced in the following subsection actually ensure strong soundness.

In
Section

7.2
, we provide sufficient conditions for soundness-must, showing that they ensure soundness as stated above (
Theorem

7.6
).
Then, in
Section

7.3
, we provide (weaker) sufficient conditions for soundness-may, and show that they ensure soundness-may (
Theorem

7.9
).

7.2.
Conditions ensuring soundness-must

The three conditions which ensure the soundness-must property are
local preservation
,
\exists
-progress
, and
\forall
-progress
.
The names suggest that the former plays the role of the
type preservation (subject reduction)
property, and the latter two of the
progress
property in small-step semantics.
However, as we will see, the correspondence is only rough, since the reasoning here is different.

Considering the first condition more closely, we use the name
preservation
rather than type preservation since, as already mentioned, the proof technique can be applied to arbitrary predicates.
More importantly,
local
means that the condition is
on single rules
rather than on the semantic relation as a whole, as standard subject reduction;
the semantic relation is only used in the hypotheses of the condition, so that, when checking it, one can rely on stronger assumptions.
The same holds for the other two conditions.

Definition 7.1 (Local preservation
(lp)
).

For each
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
in
\mathcal{R}
,
if
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}
, then there exists
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\in\mathit{I}
such that

(1)

for all
k\in 1..n
, if, for all
h<k
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
and
\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{h})\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota_{h}}
,
then
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota_{k}}
, and

(2)

if, for all
k\in 1..n
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
and
\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota_{k}}
, then
\mathit{r}\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}
.

Thinking to the paradigmatic case where the indexes are types, to check that this condition holds, for each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
where
\mathit{c}
, the conclusion, has type
\iota
,
we have to find types
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}
, which can be assigned to (configurations and results in) the premises, and,
when all the premises satisfy the chosen type,
\mathit{r}
, the result in the conclusion, must have type
\iota
, that is, the same type of
\mathit{c}
.
More precisely, we will proceed as follows:
we start finding type
\iota_{1}
, and successively find the type
\iota_{k}
for (the configuration in) the
k
-th premise assuming that all previous premises are derivable and their results have the expected types, and, finally, we have to check that the final result
\mathit{r}
has type
\iota
assuming all premises are derivable and their results have the expected type.
Indeed, if all such previous premises are derivable, then the expected type should be preserved by their results; if some premise is not derivable, the considered rule is “useless”.
For instance, considering (an instantiation of) meta-rule
{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({app})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\ \ {\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\ \ {{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}},\,\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2},\,\mathit{v})
in
Fig.

1
, we prove that
{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]
has the type
\mathit{T}
of
\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}
under the assumption that
\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}
has type
\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
, and
\mathit{v}_{2}
has type
\mathit{T}^{\prime}
(see the proof example in
Section

8.1
for more details).
A counter-example to condition
(lp)
is discussed at the beginning of
Section

8.3
.

The following lemma states that local preservation actually implies
preservation
of the semantic relation as a whole.

Lemma 7.2 (Preservation).

Let
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
and
\Pi={\langle{\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota},\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}}\rangle}_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}
satisfy condition
(lp)
.
If
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
and
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}
, then
\mathit{r}\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}
.

Proof.

The proof is by a double induction
From the hypotheses, we know that
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
has a finite derivation in
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}
.
The first induction is on the derivation of
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Suppose the last applied rule is
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
and denote by
RH
the induction hypothesis.
Then, we prove by complete arithmetic induction on
k\in 1..n
(the second induction) that
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi_{\iota_{k}}
, for all
k\in 1..n
and for some
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\in\mathit{I}
.
Let us denote by
IH
the second induction hypothesis.
By
(lp)
, there are indexes
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\in\mathit{I}
, satisfying
Items

1
and
2
of
(lp)
(cf.
Definition

7.1
). Let
k\in 1..n
, then
by
IH
we know that
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{h})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota_{h}}
, for all
h<k
.
Then, by
RH
,
we get that
\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{h})\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota_{h}}
.
Hence,
by
(lp)
(cf.
Definition

7.1
(
1
)), we get
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi_{\iota_{k}}
, as needed.

Now, since
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota_{k}}
, for all
k\in 1..n
, as we have just proved, again by
RH
, we get that
\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota_{k}}
, for all
k\in 1..n
.
Then, by
(lp)
(cf.
Definition

7.1
(
2
)), we conclude that
\mathit{r}\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}
, as needed.
∎

The following proposition is a form of local preservation where indexes (
e.g.
, specific types) are not relevant, simpler to use in the
proofs of

Theorems

7.6
and
7.9
.

Proposition 7.3.

Let
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
and
\Pi={\langle{\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota},\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}}\rangle}_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}
satisfy condition
(lp)
.
For each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
and
k\in 1..n
,
if
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
and, for all
h<k
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
, then
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis we know that
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota}
, for some
\iota\in\mathit{I}
, thus by condition
(lp)
, there are indexes
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\in\mathit{I}
, satisfying
Items

1
and
2
of
(lp)
(cf.
Definition

7.1
). We show by
complete arithmetic induction that, for all
k\in 1..n
,
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota_{k}}
, which implies the thesis.
Assume the thesis for all
h<k
,
then, since by hypothesis we have
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
for all
h<k
, we get, by induction hypothesis,
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{h})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota_{h}}
, for all
h<k
.
By
Lemma

7.2
, we also get
\mathit{R}(\mathit{j}_{h})\in\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota_{h}}
, hence, by condition
(lp)
(cf.
Definition

7.1
(
1
)), we get
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota_{k}}
, as needed.
∎

The second condition, named
\exists
-progress
, ensures that, for configurations satisfying
\Pi
(
e.g.
, well-typed),
we can
start
the evaluation, that is, the construction of an evaluation tree.

Definition 7.4 (
\exists
-progress
(
\exists
p)
).

For each

\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, there exists a rule
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
.

The third condition, named
\forall
-progress
, ensures that, for configurations satisfying

\Pi
(
e.g.
, well-typed), we can
continue
the evaluation, that is, the construction of the evaluation tree.
This condition uses the equivalence on rules introduced in
Definition

4.12
.

Definition 7.5 (
\forall
-progress
(
\forall
p)
).

For each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
with
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, for each
k\in 1..n
,
if, for all
h<k
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
and
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}
, for some
\mathit{r}^{\prime}\in\mathit{R}
,
then there is a rule
\rho^{\prime}\sim_{k}\rho
such that
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},k)=\mathit{r}^{\prime}
.

We have to check, for each rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
, the following:
if the configuration
\mathit{c}
in the conclusion satisfies the predicate (
e.g.
, is well-typed), then, for each
k\in 1..n
,
if the configuration in the
k
-th premise evaluates to some result
\mathit{r}^{\prime}
(that is,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}
),
then there is a rule (
\rho
itself or another rule with the same configuration in the conclusion and the same first
k-1
premises) with such judgement as
k
-th premise.
This check can be done under the assumption that all the previous premises are derivable.
For instance, consider again (an instantiation of) the meta-rule
{\scriptstyle{\textsc{({app})}}}\ {\mathsf{rule}}({\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\ \ {\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}\ \ {{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}},\,\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2},\,\mathit{v})
.
Assuming that
\mathit{e}_{1}
evaluates to some
\mathit{v}_{1}
, we have to check that there is a rule with first premise
{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{1}}
, in practice, that
\mathit{v}_{1}
is a
\lambda
-abstraction;
in general, checking
(
\forall
p)
for a (meta-)rule amounts to show that configurations in the premises evaluate to results with the required shape (see also the proof example in
Section

8.1
).

We now prove the claim of soundness-must expressed by means of the
wrong
construction (cf.
Section

5.2
).

Theorem 7.6 (Soundness-must).

Let
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
and
\Pi={\langle{\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota},\Pi^{\mathit{R}}_{\iota}}\rangle}_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}
satisfy conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
.
If
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.

Proof.

To prove the statement, we assume
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
and look for a contradiction.
The proof is by induction on the derivation of
{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
.
We split cases on the last applied rule in such derivation.

Case:
{\textsf{wrong}}(\mathit{c})

By construction (cf.
Definition

5.5
), we know that there is no rule
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, and this violates condition
(
\exists
p)
, since
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, by hypothesis.

Case:
{\textsf{wrong}}(\rho,\,i,\,\mathit{r}^{\prime})

Suppose
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
, hence
i\in 1..n
,
then, by hypothesis, for all
k<i
, we have
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}^{\prime}}
, and these judgments can also be derived in
\mathcal{R}
by conservativity (cf.
Theorem

5.6
).
Furthermore, by construction (cf.
Definition

5.5
), we know that there is no other rule
\rho^{\prime}\sim_{i}\rho
such that
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime},i)=\mathit{r}^{\prime}
, and this violates condition
(
\forall
p)
, since
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
by hypothesis.

Case:
{\mathsf{prop}}(\rho,i,\textsf{wrong})

Suppose
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
, hence
i\in 1..n
,
then, by hypothesis, for all
k<i
, we have
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
,
and these judgments
can also be derived in
\mathcal{R}
by conservativity (cf.
Theorem

5.6
).
Then, by
Proposition

7.3
(which requires condition
(lp)
), since
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, we have

\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, hence we get the thesis by induction hypothesis, because
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{wr}}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{i})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\textsf{wrong}}
holds by hypothesis.

∎

Note that conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
, actually ensure strong soundness, because, by
Lemma

7.2
, which is applicable since we assume
(lp)
, we have that converging computations preserve indexes of the predicate.

7.3.
Conditions ensuring soundness-may

As discussed in
Section

7.1
, if we explicitly model divergence rather than stuck computations, we can only express a weaker form of soundness: at least one computation is not stuck (
soundness-may
).
Actually, we will state soundness-may in a different, but equivalent, way, which is simpler to prove, that is, a configuration that does not converge, diverges.

As the reader can expect, to ensure this property weaker sufficient conditions are enough: namely, condition
(lp)
, and another condition,
named
may-progress
, defined below.
We write “
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}
” if
\mathit{c}

does not converge
(there is no
\mathit{r}
such that
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
).

Definition 7.7 (May-progress
(mayp)
).

For each
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, there is a rule
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
such that,
if there is a (first)
k\in 1..n

such that
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
and,
for all
h<k
,

\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
,
then
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}
.

This condition can be informally understood as follows:
we have to show that there is an either finite or infinite computation for
\mathit{c}
.
If we find a rule where all premises are derivable (there is no
k
), then there is a finite computation.
Otherwise,
\mathit{c}
cannot converge.
In this case, we should find a rule where the configuration in the first non-derivable premise
k
cannot converge as well.
Indeed, by coinductive reasoning (cf.
Theorem

7.9
), this implies that
\mathit{c}
diverges.
The following proposition states that this condition is indeed a weakening of
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
.

Proposition 7.8.

Conditions
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
imply condition
(mayp)
.

Proof.

For each
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}
, let us define
b_{\mathit{c}}\in\mathbb{N}
as
\max\{\#\rho\mid\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}\}
, which is well-defined and finite by condition (
BP
) in
Definition

3.1
.
For each rule
\rho
with
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, let us denote by
nd(\rho)
the index of the first premise of
\rho
which is not derivable, if any,
otherwise set
nd(\rho)=b_{\mathit{c}}+1
.
For each
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, we first prove the following fact:

(\star)
for each rule
\rho
, with
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, there exists a rule
\rho^{\prime}
such that
\mathit{C}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathit{c}
,
nd(\rho^{\prime})\geq nd(\rho)
and,
if
nd(\rho^{\prime})\leq b_{\mathit{c}}
, then
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\rho^{\prime},nd(\rho^{\prime}))}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}
.
Note that the requirement in
(\star)
is the same as that of condition
(mayp)
.
The proof is by complete arithmetic induction on
h(\rho)=b_{\mathit{c}}+1-nd(\rho)
.
If
h(\rho)=0
, hence
nd(\rho)=b_{\mathit{c}}+1
, then the thesis follows by taking
\rho^{\prime}=\rho
.
Otherwise, we have two cases:
if there is no
\mathit{r}\in\mathit{R}
such that
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\rho,nd(\rho))}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
, then we have the thesis taking
\rho^{\prime}=\rho
;
otherwise, by condition
(
\forall
p)
, there is a rule
\rho^{\prime\prime}\sim_{nd(\rho)}\rho
such that
\mathit{R}(\rho^{\prime\prime},nd(\rho))=\mathit{r}
, hence
nd(\rho^{\prime\prime})>nd(\rho)
.
Then, we have
h(\rho^{\prime\prime})<h(\rho)
, hence we get the thesis by induction hypothesis.

Now, by
(
\exists
p)
, there is a rule
\rho
with
\mathit{C}(\rho)=\mathit{c}
, and applying
(\star)
to
\rho
we get
(mayp)
.
∎

We now prove the claim of soundness-may expressed by means of the divergence construction (cf.
Section

6
).

Theorem 7.9 (Soundness-may).

Let
\langle{\mathit{C},\mathit{R},\mathcal{R}}\rangle
and
\Pi={\langle{\Pi^{\mathit{C}}_{\iota},\mathit{R}_{\iota}}\rangle}_{\iota\in\mathit{I}}
satisfy conditions
(lp)
and
(mayp)
.
If
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, then
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}_{\infty}}
, for some
\mathit{r}_{\infty}\in\mathit{R}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

First note that, thanks to
Theorem

6.3
, the statement is equivalent to the following:

If
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
and
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}
, then
{\langle{\mathcal{R}_{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{co}}}\rangle}\vdash_{\nu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\infty}
.

Then, the thesis follows by
Lemma

6.4
.
We set
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}\mid\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}\}
, and show that, for all
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
,
there are
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
and
k\in 1..n
such that, for all
h<k
,
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
and
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\mathcal{S}
.

Consider
\mathit{c}\in\mathcal{S}
, then, by
(mayp)
(cf.
Definition

7.7
), there is
\rho={\mathsf{rule}}(\mathit{j}_{1}\ldots\mathit{j}_{n},\,\mathit{c},\,\mathit{r})
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
, we have
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}
,
hence there exists a (first)
k\in 1..n+1
such that
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{k}
,
since, otherwise, we would have
