\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{c}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{r}}
.
Then, since
k
is the first index with such property, for all
h<k
, we have
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
, hence, again by condition
(mayp)
(cf.
Definition

7.7
), we have that
\mathcal{R}\not\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{}
.
Finally, since
\mathit{c}\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
and, for all
h<k
, we have
\mathcal{R}\vdash_{\mu}\mathit{j}_{h}
, by
Proposition

7.3
we get
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\Pi^{\mathit{C}}
, hence
\mathit{C}(\mathit{j}_{k})\in\mathcal{S}
, as needed.
∎

Note that conditions
(lp)
and
(mayp)
actually ensure strong soundness, because, by
Lemma

7.2
, which is applicable since we assume
(lp)
, we have that converging computations preserve indexes of the predicate.

8.
Examples of soundness proofs

In this section, we show how to use the technique introduced in
Section

7
to prove soundness of a type system with respect to a big-step semantics, by several examples.
We focus on the technique for soundness-must, as it is the usual notion of soundness for type systems.

Section

8.1
explains in detail how a typical soundness proof can be rephrased in terms of our technique, by reasoning directly on big-step rules.

Section

8.2
shows a case where this is advantageous, since the property to be checked is
not preserved
by intermediate computation steps, whereas it holds for the whole computation.

Section

8.3
considers a more sophisticated type system, with intersection and union types.

Section

8.4
shows another example where types are not preserved, whereas soundness can be proved with our technique.
This example is intended as a preliminary step towards a more challenging case.
In
Section

8.5
we show how our technique can also deal with imperative features.

8.1.
Simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus with recursive types

As a first example, we take the
\lambda
-calculus with natural constants, successor, and non-deterministic choice introduced in
Fig.

1
.
We consider a standard simply-typed version with (equi)recursive types, obtained by interpreting the production in the top section of
Fig.

8

coinductively.
Introducing recursive types makes the calculus non-normalising and permits
to write interesting programs such as
\Omega
(see
Section

5.1
).

The typing rules are recalled in the bottom section of
Fig.

8
and, as usual, they are interpreted inductively.
Type environments, written
\Gamma
, are finite maps from variables to types, and
\Gamma\{\mathit{T}/\mathit{x}\}
denotes the map which returns
\mathit{T}
on
\mathit{x}
and coincides with
\Gamma
elsewhere.
We write
\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
for
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{T}&::=&\mathtt{Nat}\mid\mathit{T}_{1}\rightarrow\mathit{T}_{2}&\mbox{types}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathit{T}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-const)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{n}:\mathtt{Nat}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-abs)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\{\mathit{T}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-app)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-succ)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Nat}}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Nat}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-choice)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 8.
\lambda
-calculus: type system

Let
\langle{\mathit{C}_{1},\mathit{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}}\rangle
be the big-step semantics described in
Fig.

1
(
\mathit{C}_{1}
is the set of expressions and
\mathit{R}_{1}
is the set of values), and let

{\Pi 1}^{\mathit{C}}_{T}=\{\mathit{e}\in\mathit{C}_{1}\mid\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\}
and

{\Pi 1}^{\mathit{R}}_{T}=\{\mathit{v}\in\mathit{R}_{1}\mid\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}\}
,
where
\mathit{T}
is a type, defined in
Fig.

8
, that is,

{\Pi 1}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{T}}
and
{\Pi 1}^{\mathit{R}}_{\mathit{T}}
are the sets of expressions and values of type
\mathit{T}
, respectively.
To prove the three conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
of
Section

7.2
, we need lemmas of inversion, substitution and canonical forms, as in the standard technique for small-step semantics.

Lemma 8.1 (Inversion).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathit{T}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{n}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{T}=\mathtt{Nat}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{T}=\mathit{T}_{1}\rightarrow\mathit{T}_{2}
and
\Gamma\{\mathit{T}_{1}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}_{2}
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
.

(5)

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{T}=\mathtt{Nat}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Nat}
.

(6)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}
with
i\in 1,2
.

Lemma 8.2 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma\{\mathit{T}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{e}^{\prime}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathit{T}
.

Lemma 8.3 (Canonical Forms).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{v}=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}
.

(2)

If
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathtt{Nat}
, then
\mathit{v}=\mathit{n}
.

Theorem 8.4 (Soundness).

The big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C}_{1},\mathit{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}}\rangle
and the indexed predicate
{\Pi 1}
satisfy the conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
of
Section

7.2
.

Proof.

Since the aim of this first example is to illustrate the proof technique, we provide a proof where we explain the reasoning in detail.

Proof of
(lp)
:
We should prove this condition for each (instantiation of meta-)rule in
Fig.

1
.

Case:
(app)

Assume that
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
holds.
We have to find types for the premises.
We proceed as follows:

(1)

First premise: by
Lemma

8.1
(
4
),
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
.

(2)

Second premise: again by
Lemma

8.1
(
4
),
\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
(without needing the assumption
\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
).

(3)

Third premise:
\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathit{T}
should hold (assuming
\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
,
\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
).
Since
\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
, by
Lemma

8.1
(
3
) we have
\mathit{x}{:}\mathit{T}^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, so by
Lemma

8.2
and
\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
we have
\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathit{T}
.

Finally, we have to show
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}
, assuming
\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
,
\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
and
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}
, which is trivial from the third assumption.

Case:
(succ)

Assume that
\vdash\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
holds.
By
Lemma

8.1
(
5
),
\mathit{T}=\mathtt{Nat}
, and
\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Nat}
, hence we find
\mathtt{Nat}
as type for the premise.
Moreover,
\vdash\mathit{n}+1:\mathtt{Nat}
holds by rule
(t-const)
.

Case:
(choice)

Assume that
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
holds.
By
Lemma

8.1
(
6
), we have
\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}
, with
i\in 1,2
.
Hence we find
\mathit{T}
as type for the premise.
Finally, we have to show
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}
, assuming
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}
, which is trivial.

Case:
(val)

Trivial by assumption.

Proof of
(
\exists
p)
:
We should prove that, for each configuration (here, expression
\mathit{e}
) such that
\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
holds for some
\mathit{T}
, there is a rule with this configuration in the conclusion.
The expression
\mathit{e}
cannot be a variable, since a variable cannot be typed in the empty environment.
Application, successor, choice, abstraction and constants appear as consequence in the big-step rules
(app)
,
(succ)
,
(choice)
and
(val)
.

Proof of
(
\forall
p)
:
We should prove this condition for each (instantiation of meta-)rule.

Case:
(app)

Assuming
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
, again by
Lemma

8.1
(
4
) we get
{\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}}
.

(1)

First premise: if
{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
is derivable, then there should be a rule with
\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}
in the conclusion and
{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
as first premise.
Since we proved
(lp)
, by preservation (
Lemma

7.2
)

\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
holds.
Then, by
Lemma

8.3
(
1
),
\mathit{v}
has shape
\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}
, hence the required rule exists.
As noted at page
7.2
, in practice checking
(
\forall
p)
for a (meta-)rule amounts to show that configurations in the premises evaluate to results which have the required shape (to be a
\lambda
-abstraction in this case).

(2)

Second premise: if
{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}
, and
{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
, then there should be a rule with
\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}
in the conclusion and
{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}
,
{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
as first two premises.
This is trivial since the meta-variable
\mathit{v}_{2}
can be freely instantiated in the meta-rule.

(3)

Third premise: trivial as the previous one.

Case:
(succ)

Assuming
\vdash\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, again by
Lemma

8.1
(
5
) we get
\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Nat}
.
If
{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
is derivable, there should be a rule with
\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}
in the conclusion and
{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
as first premise.
Indeed, by preservation (
Lemma

7.2
) and
Lemma

8.3
(
2
),
\mathit{v}
has shape
\mathit{n}
.

Case:
(choice)

Trivial since the meta-variable
\mathit{v}
can be freely instantiated.

Case:
(val)

Empty, because there are no premises.

∎

An interesting remark is that, differently from the standard approach, there is
no induction
in the proof: everything is
by cases
.
This is a consequence of the fact that, as discussed in
Section

7.2
, the three conditions are
local
, that is, they are conditions on single rules.
Induction is “hidden” once and for all in the proof that those three conditions are sufficient to ensure soundness.

If we drop in
Fig.

1
rule
(succ)
, then condition
(
\exists
p)
fails, since there is no longer a rule for the well-typed configuration
\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{n}
.
If we add the
(fool)
rule
\vdash 0\,0:\mathtt{Nat}
, then condition
(
\forall
p)
fails for rule
(app)
, since
{0}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{0}
is derivable, but there is no rule with
0\,0
in the conclusion and
{0}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{0}
as first premise.

8.2.
\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda

In this example, the language is a subset of
\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda

(
Bettini et al. 2018
)
, a calculus extending Featherweight Java (
FJ
) with
\lambda
-abstractions and intersection types, introduced in Java 8.
To keep the example small, we do not consider intersections and focus on one key typing feature:
\lambda
-abstractions can only be typed when occurring in a context requiring a given type (called the
target type
).
In a small-step semantics, this poses a problem: reduction can move
\lambda
-abstractions into arbitrary contexts, leading to intermediate terms which would be ill-typed.
To maintain subject reduction,
Bettini et al. 2018
decorate
\lambda
-abstractions with their initial target type.
In a big-step semantics, there is no need of intermediate terms and annotations.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{e}&::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}\mid\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}\mid\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}\mid\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}\mid\texttt{(}\mathit{T}\texttt{)}\mathit{e}&\mbox{expression}\\
\mathit{T}&::=&\mathsf{C}\mid\mathsf{I}&\mbox{type}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{c}&::=&{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}&\mbox{configuration}\\
\mathit{v}&::=&[\mathit{vs}]^{\mathsf{C}}\mid\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}&\mbox{result (value)}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{x}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\textsc{e}(\mathit{x})=\mathit{v}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(new)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(field-access)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}}}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\\
\end{array}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{[{\mathit{vs}}]^{\mathsf{C}}}\\
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n\\
{{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{v}_{n},\texttt{this}{:}[\mathit{vs}]^{\mathsf{C}},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}\end{array}}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}={\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{($\lambda$-invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n}.\mathit{e}}\\
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n\\
{{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{v}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}\end{array}}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{($\lambda$)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(upcast)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}{{{\langle{\textsc{e},\texttt{(}\mathit{T}\texttt{)}\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 9.
\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda
: syntax and big-step semantics

The syntax is given in the first part of
Fig.

9
.
We assume sets of
variables

\mathit{x}
,
class names

\mathsf{C}
,
interface names

\mathsf{I}
,
\mathsf{J}
,
field names

\mathsf{f}
, and
method names

\mathsf{m}
.
As usual, we assume a special variable
this
, used in method bodies to refer to the receiver object.
Interfaces which have
exactly
one method (dubbed
functional interfaces
) can be used as target
types.
Expressions are those of
FJ
, plus
\lambda
-abstractions, and types are class and interface names.
Throughout this section
\mathit{xs}
and
\mathit{vs}
denote lists of variables and values, respectively.
In
\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}
we assume that
\mathit{xs}
is not empty and
\mathit{e}
is not a
\lambda
-abstraction.
For simplicity, we only consider
upcasts
, which have no runtime effect, but are important to allow the programmer to use
\lambda
-abstractions,
as exemplified in discussing typing rules.

To be concise, the class table is abstractly modelled as follows:

•

\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})
gives the sequence of field declarations
\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}..\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
for class
\mathsf{C}

•

{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T},\mathsf{m})}
gives, for each method
\mathsf{m}
in class or interface
\mathit{T}
, the pair
\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}

consisting of the parameter types and return type

•

{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}
gives, for each method
\mathsf{m}
in class
\mathsf{C}
, the pair
{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}

consisting of the parameters and body

•

<:
is the reflexive and transitive closure of the union of the
\mathsf{extends}
and
\mathsf{implements}
relations, stating that two class or interface names are related iff they occur in the class table connected by the keywords
\mathsf{extends}
or
\mathsf{implements}

•

!{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{I})}
gives, for each
functional
interface
\mathsf{I}
,
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{I},\mathsf{m})}
, where
\mathsf{m}
is the only method of
\mathsf{I}
.

The big-step semantics is given in the last part of
Fig.

9
.
\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda
shows an example of instantiation of the framework where configurations include an auxiliary structure, rather than being just language terms.
In this case, the structure is an
environment

e
(a finite map from variables to values) modelling the current stack frame.
Furthermore, results are not particular configurations: they are either
objects
, of shape
[\mathit{vs}]^{\mathsf{C}}
, or
\lambda
-abstractions.

Rules for
FJ
constructs are straightforward.
Note that, since we only consider upcasts, casts have no runtime effect.
Indeed, they are guaranteed to succeed on well-typed expressions.
Rule
(
\lambda
-invk)
shows that, when the receiver of a method is a
\lambda
-abstraction, the method name is not significant at runtime, and the effect is that the body of the function is evaluated as in the usual application.

The type system, consisting of judgements for configurations, expressions and values, is given in
Fig.

10
.
The following assumptions formalize standard
FJ
typing constraints on the class table.

(
\textsc{FJ}1
):

Method bodies are well-typed with respect to method types:

•:

either
{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}
and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}
are both undefined

•:

or
{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}={\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}
,

{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
, and
{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n},\texttt{this}{:}\mathsf{C}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}
.

(
\textsc{FJ}2
):

Fields are inherited, no field hiding:

if
\mathit{T}<:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathit{T}^{\prime})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
, then

\mathsf{fields}(\mathit{T})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{m}\,\mathsf{f}_{m}\texttt{;}
,
m\geq n
, and
\mathsf{f}_{i}\neq\mathsf{f}_{j}
for
i\neq j
.

(
\textsc{FJ}3
):

Methods are inherited, no method overloading, invariant overriding:

if
\mathit{T}<:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
, and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}^{\prime},\mathsf{m})}
is defined, then
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T},\mathsf{m})}={\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}^{\prime},\mathsf{m})}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-conf)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n\hskip 6.47491pt\hskip 6.47491pt\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{n}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\vdash{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{v}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{T}_{i}<:\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathit{T}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-upcast)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{(}\mathit{T}\texttt{)}\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-field-access)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}:\mathit{T}_{i}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-new)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
\end{array}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 0..n}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{e}_{0}\ \mbox{not of shape}\ \lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}\\
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}\\
\end{array}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-$\lambda$)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n}.\mathit{e}:\mathsf{I}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}!{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{I})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}\end{array}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-object)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma\vdash[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}:\mathsf{C}{}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n\end{array}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-sub)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{e}\ \mbox{not of shape}\ \lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}\\
\mathit{T}<:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 10.
\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda
: type system

Besides the standard typing features of
FJ
, the
\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda
type system ensures the following.

•

A functional interface
\mathsf{I}
can be assigned as type to a
\lambda
-abstraction which has the functional type of the method,
see rule
(t-
\lambda
)
.

•

A
\lambda
-abstraction should have a
target type
determined by the context where the
\lambda
-abstraction occurs.
More precisely, as described by
Gosling et al. 2014
, a
\lambda
-abstraction in our calculus can only occur as return expression of a method or argument of constructor, method call or cast.
Then, in some contexts a
\lambda
-abstraction cannot be typed,
in our calculus when occurring as receiver in field access or method invocation, hence these cases should be prevented.
This is implicit in rule
(t-field-access)
, since the type of the receiver should be a class name, whereas it is explicitly forbidden in rule
(t-invk)
.
Finally,
a
\lambda
-abstraction cannot be the main expression of a program, as also in this case the target type is not well defined.
For simplicity, this requirement is not enforced by typing rules, but it can be easily recovered as an assumption on the source program.

•

A
\lambda
-abstraction with a given target type
\mathsf{J}
should have type
exactly

\mathsf{J}
: a subtype
\mathsf{I}
of
\mathsf{J}
is not enough.
Consider, for instance, the following class table:

⬇

interface

J

{}

interface

I

extends

J

{

A

m
(
A

x
);

}

class

C

{

J

f
;

}

class

D

{

D

m
(
I

y
)

{

return

new

D
().
n
(
y
);

}

D

n
(
J

y
)

{

return

new

D
();

}

}

In the main expression
\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\texttt{)}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{n}\texttt{(}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}
,
the
\lambda
-abstraction has target type
\mathsf{J}
, which is
not
a functional interface, hence the expression is ill-typed in Java (the compiler has no functional type against which to typecheck the
\lambda
-abstraction). On the other hand, in the body of method
\mathsf{m}
, the parameter
y
of type
\mathsf{I}
can be passed, as usual, to method
\mathsf{n}
expecting a supertype. For instance, the main expression
\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\texttt{)}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}
is well-typed, since the
\lambda
-abstraction has target type
\mathsf{I}
, and can be safely passed to method
\mathsf{n}
, since it is not used as function there. To formalise this behaviour, it is forbidden to apply subsumption to
\lambda
-abstractions, see rule
(t-sub)
.

•

However,
\lambda
-abstractions occurring as results rather than in source code (that is, in the environment and as fields of objects) are allowed to have a subtype of the required type, see the explicit side condition in rules
(t-conf)
and
(t-object)
.
For instance, in the above class table,
the expression

\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\texttt{(}\mathsf{I}\texttt{)}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}
is well-typed, whereas
\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}
is ill typed, since rule
(t-sub)
cannot be applied to
\lambda
-abstractions. When the expression is evaluated, the result is
[\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}]^{\mathsf{C}}
, which is well-typed.

As mentioned at the beginning, the obvious small-step semantics would produce not typable expressions. In the above example, we get

\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\texttt{(}\mathsf{I}\texttt{)}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}\longrightarrow\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}\longrightarrow[\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}]^{\mathsf{C}}

and
\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}
has no type, while
\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\texttt{(}\mathsf{I}\texttt{)}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\texttt{)}
and
[\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}]^{\mathsf{C}}
have type
\mathsf{C}
.

As expected, to show soundness (
Theorem

8.7
) lemmas of inversion and canonical forms are handy: they can be easily proved as usual.
Instead, we do not need a substitution lemma, since environments associate variables with values.

Lemma 8.5 (Inversion).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\vdash{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{v}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
,
\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma(\mathit{x})<:\mathit{T}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\mathit{T}_{i}<:T
where

i\in 1..n
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathsf{C}<:\mathit{T}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.

(5)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{e}_{0}
not of shape
\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}
and

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
.

(6)

If
\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{T}=\mathsf{I}
and
!{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{I})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
.

(7)

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{(}\mathit{T}^{\prime}\texttt{)}\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
.

(8)

If
\Gamma\vdash[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}:\mathit{T}{}
, then
\mathsf{C}<:\mathit{T}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.

Lemma 8.6 (Canonical Forms).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathsf{C}
, then
\mathit{v}=[\mathit{vs}]^{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{D}<:\mathsf{C}
.

(2)

If
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathsf{I}
, then either
\mathit{v}=[\mathit{vs}]^{\mathsf{C}}
and
\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{I}
or
\mathit{v}=\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}
and
\mathsf{I}
is a functional interface.

We write
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:<:\mathit{T}
as short for
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
for some
\mathit{T}^{\prime}
.
In order to state soundness, set
\langle{\mathit{C}_{2},\mathit{R}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2}}\rangle
the big-step semantics defined in
Fig.

9
, and
let
{\Pi 2}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{T}}=\{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}\in\mathit{C}_{2}\mid\vdash{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:<:\mathit{T}\}
and

{\Pi 2}^{\mathit{R}}_{\mathit{T}}=\{\mathit{v}\in\mathit{R}_{2}\mid\vdash\mathit{v}:<:\mathit{T}\}
, for
\mathit{T}
defined in
Fig.

9
.

Theorem 8.7 (Soundness).

The big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C}_{2},\mathit{R}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2}}\rangle
and the indexed predicate
{\Pi 2}
satisfy the conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
of
Section

7.2
.

Proof.

Proof of
(lp)
:
The proof is by cases on instantiations of meta-rules.
In all such cases, we have a configuration
{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}
in the conclusion, with
\textsc{e}=y_{1}{:}\hat{\mathit{v}}_{1},\ldots,y_{p}{:}\hat{\mathit{v}}_{p}
, such that

\vdash{\langle{y_{1}{:}\hat{\mathit{v}}_{1},\ldots,y_{p}{:}\hat{\mathit{v}}_{p},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:<:\hat{\mathit{T}}
, hence,
by
Lemma

8.5
(
1
), we get
\vdash\hat{\mathit{v}}_{\ell}:<:\hat{\mathit{T}}_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in 1..p
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
with
\Gamma=y_{1}{:}\hat{\mathit{T}}_{1},\ldots,y_{p}{:}\hat{\mathit{T}}_{p}
and
\mathit{T}<:\hat{\mathit{T}}
, for some
\hat{\mathit{T}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathit{T}}_{p}
.

Case:
(var)

Lemma

8.5
(
2
) applied to
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}
implies
\mathit{x}=y_{i}
and
\hat{\mathit{T}}_{i}<:\mathit{T}
for some
i\in 1..p
.
Then, the thesis follows by transitivity of subtyping since
\textsc{e}(\mathit{x})=\hat{\mathit{v}}_{i}
and
\vdash\hat{\mathit{v}}_{i}:<:\hat{\mathit{T}}_{i}
.

Case:
(field-access)

Lemma

8.5
(
3
) applied to
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathit{T}
implies
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{D})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{m}\,\mathsf{f}_{m}\texttt{;}
and
\mathit{T}_{i}<:T
where

i\in 1..m
.
Since
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}}
is a premise we assume
\vdash[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}:<:\mathsf{D}
, which implies
\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}^{\prime}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}^{\prime}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}_{j}:<:\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\in 1..n
by
Lemma

8.5
(
8
). From
\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{D}
and assumption (
\textsc{FJ}2
) we have
m\leq n
and
\mathit{T}_{j}=\mathit{T}^{\prime}_{j}
and
\mathsf{f}_{j}=\mathsf{f}^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\in 1..m
.
We conclude
\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:<:\mathit{T}
.

Case:
(new)

Lemma

8.5
(
4
) applied to
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
implies
\mathsf{C}<:\mathit{T}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Since
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}
is a premise we assume
\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:<:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Using rule
(t-object)
we derive
\vdash[\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}]^{\mathsf{C}}:<:\mathit{T}
.

Case:
(invk)

Lemma

8.5
(
5
) applied to
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
implies
\mathit{e}_{0}
not of shape
\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}
and

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
.
Since
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{[\mathit{vs}^{\prime}]^{\mathsf{C}}}
is a premise we assume
\vdash[\mathit{vs}^{\prime}]^{\mathsf{C}}:<:\mathit{T}_{0}
, which implies
\mathsf{C}<:\mathit{T}_{0}
by
Lemma

8.5
(
8
).
Since
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}
is a premise we assume

\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:<:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
We have
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
since
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{C}<:\mathit{T}_{0}
by assumption (
\textsc{FJ}3
).
By assumption (
\textsc{FJ}1
),
x_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n},\texttt{this}{:}\mathsf{C}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, by rule
(t-conf)
and since
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
, we can derive
\vdash{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{v}_{n},\texttt{this}{:}[\mathit{vs}^{\prime}]^{\mathsf{C}},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:<:\mathit{T}
.

Case:
(
\lambda
-invk)

Lemma

8.5
(
5
) applied to
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
implies
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathit{T}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
.
Since
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}}
is a premise we assume
\vdash\lambda\mathit{xs}.\mathit{e}:<:\mathit{T}_{0}
, which implies
\mathsf{I}<:\mathit{T}_{0}
and
!{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{I})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
by
Lemma

8.5
(
6
).
Since
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}
is a premise we assume

\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:<:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Therefore we derive
\vdash{\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{v}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:<:\mathit{T}
.

Case:
(
\lambda
)

The thesis is trivial as the configuration and the final result are the same.

Case:
(upcast)

Lemma

8.5
(
7
) applied to
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{(}\mathit{T}^{\prime}\texttt{)}\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
implies
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:<:\mathit{T}
.
From
{{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
we conclude
\vdash\mathit{v}:<:\mathit{T}
.

Proof of
(
\exists
p)
:
It is easy to verify that if
\vdash{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:<:\mathit{T}
, then there is a rule in
Fig.

9
, whose conclusion is
{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}
, just because for every syntactic construct there is a corresponding rule and side conditions in typing rules imply those of big-step rules.
The only less trivial case is that of variables:
if
\vdash{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{x}}\rangle}:<:\mathit{T}
, then by
Lemma

8.5
(
1
,
2
),
\mathit{x}\in\textsf{dom}(\textsc{e})
, hence rule
(var)
is applicable, as the side condition is satisfied.

Proof of
(
\forall
p)
:
Rule
(field-access)
requires that
{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}}\rangle}
reduces to an object with a field
\mathsf{f}_{i}
, and this is assured by the typing rule
(t-field-access)
, which prescribes a class type for the expression
\mathit{e}
with the field
\mathsf{f}_{i}
, together with the validity of condition
(lp)
(which assures type preservation by
Lemma

7.2
) and
Lemma

8.6
(
1
).
For a well-typed method call
\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}
the configuration
{\langle{\textsc{e},\mathit{e}_{0}}\rangle}
can reduce either to an object or to a
\lambda
-expression.
In the first case we can apply rule
(invk)
and in the second case rule
(
\lambda
-invk)
.
In both cases the typing assures that the arguments are in the right number, while the condition is trivial for the last premise.
∎

8.3.
Intersection and union types

We enrich the type system of
Fig.

8
by adding intersection and union type constructors and the corresponding typing rules, see
Fig.

11
.
Intersection types for the
\lambda
-calculus have been widely studied,
e.g.
, by
Barendregt
et al. 2013
.
Union types naturally model conditionals
(
Grudzinski 2000
)
and non-deterministic choice
(
Dezani-Ciancaglini et al. 1998
)
.

The production in the top section of
Fig.

11
is again interpreted coinductively to allow possibly infinite types, but,
as usual with recursive types, we only consider
contractive
types
(
Pierce 2002
)
, that is, we require an infinite number of arrows in each infinite path in a type (viewed as a tree).
On the other hand, typing rules are still interpreted inductively.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{T}&::=&\mathtt{Nat}\mid\mathit{T}_{1}\to\mathit{T}_{2}\mid\mathit{T}_{1}\wedge\mathit{T}_{2}\mid\mathit{T}_{1}\vee\mathit{T}_{2}&\mbox{type}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{($\wedge$ I)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{S}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\wedge\mathit{S}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{($\wedge$ E)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\wedge\mathit{S}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{($\wedge$ E)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\wedge\mathit{S}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{S}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{($\vee$ I)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\vee\mathit{S}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{($\vee$ I)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{S}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\vee\mathit{S}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 11.
Intersection and union types: syntax and typing rules

The typing rules for the introduction and the elimination of intersection and union are standard, except for the absence of the union elimination rule:

\textsc{\scriptsize{($\vee E$)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\{\mathit{T}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:V\hskip 6.99997pt\Gamma\{\mathit{S}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:V\hskip 6.99997pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathit{T}\vee\mathit{S}}{\Gamma\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{e}^{\prime}}/{\mathit{x}}]:V}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}

As a matter of fact, rule
(
\vee E
)
is unsound for
\oplus
.
For example, let split the type
\mathtt{Nat}
into
\mathtt{Even}
and
\mathtt{Odd}
and add the expected typings for natural numbers.
The prefix addition
\mathtt{+}
has type

(\mathtt{Even}\rightarrow\mathtt{Even}\rightarrow\mathtt{Even})\wedge(\mathtt{Odd}\rightarrow\mathtt{Odd}\rightarrow\mathtt{Even})

and we derive

\footnotesize\frac{x{:}\mathtt{Even}\vdash\!+\,x\,x{:}\mathtt{Even}\hskip 5.95006ptx{:}\mathtt{Odd}\vdash\!+\,x\,x{:}\mathtt{Even}\hskip 5.95006pt\frac{\frac{\vdash 1:\mathtt{Odd}}{\vdash 1:\mathtt{Even}\vee\mathtt{Odd}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\hskip 5.77115pt\frac{\vdash 2:\mathtt{Even}}{\vdash 2:\mathtt{Even}\vee\mathtt{Odd}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\vdash(1\oplus 2):\mathtt{Even}\vee\mathtt{Odd}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\vdash{+}(1\oplus 2)(1\oplus 2):\mathtt{Even}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}

We cannot assign the type
\mathtt{Even}
to
3
, which is a possible result, so strong soundness is lost.
In addition, in the small-step approach, we cannot assign
\mathtt{Even}
to the intermediate term
{+}\,1\,2
, so subject reduction fails.
In the big-step approach, there is no such intermediate term; however, condition
(lp)
fails for the big-step rule for
+
.
Indeed, considering the following instantiation of the rule:

\textsc{\scriptsize{($+$)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{1\oplus 2}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{1}\hskip 6.99997pt{1\oplus 2}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{2}}{{{+}(1\oplus 2)(1\oplus 2)}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{3}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}

and the type
\mathtt{Even}
for the conclusion: we cannot assign this type to the final result as required by
(lp)
(cf.
Definition

7.1
(
2
)).

Intersection types allow to derive meaningful types also for expressions containing variables applied to themselves, for example we can derive

\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x}:(\mathit{T}\rightarrow\mathit{S})\wedge\mathit{T}\rightarrow\mathit{S}
.
With union types all non-deterministic choices between typable expressions can be typed too, since we can derive
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}_{1}\vee\mathit{T}_{2}
from
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}_{2}
.

We now state standard lemmas for the type system, which are handy towards the soundness proof.
We first define the
subtyping
relation
\mathit{T}\leq\mathit{S}
as the smallest preorder such that:

•

\mathit{S}\leq\mathit{T}_{1}
and
\mathit{S}\leq\mathit{T}_{2}
imply
\mathit{S}\leq\mathit{T}_{1}\wedge\mathit{T}_{2}
;

•

\mathit{T}\wedge\mathit{S}\leq\mathit{T}
and
\mathit{T}\wedge\mathit{S}\leq\mathit{S}
;

•

\mathit{T}\leq\mathit{T}\vee\mathit{S}
and
\mathit{T}\leq\mathit{S}\vee\mathit{T}
.

It is easy to verify that
\mathit{T}\leq\mathit{S}
iff
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathit{T}\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{S}
for an arbitrary variable
\mathit{x}
, using rules
(
\wedge
I)
,
(
\wedge
E)
and
(
\vee
I)
.

Lemma 8.8 (Inversion).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma(\mathit{x})\leq\mathit{T}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{n}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathtt{Nat}\leq\mathit{T}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\{\mathit{S}_{i}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:V_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
and

\bigwedge_{i\in 1..m}(\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i})\leq\mathit{T}
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{S}_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
and
\bigwedge_{i\in 1..m}V_{i}\leq\mathit{T}
.

(5)

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{succ}\,\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathtt{Nat}\leq\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Nat}
.

(6)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\oplus\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{T}^{\prime}\leq\mathit{T}
and
i\in 1..2
.

Lemma 8.9 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma\{\mathit{T}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{e}^{\prime}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathit{T}
.

Lemma 8.10 (Canonical Forms).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathit{T}
, then
\mathit{v}=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}
.

(2)

If
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathtt{Nat}
, then
\mathit{v}=\mathit{n}
.

In order to state soundness, let

{\Pi 3}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{T}}=\{\mathit{e}\in\mathit{C}_{1}\mid\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\}
and

{\Pi 3}^{\mathit{R}}_{\mathit{T}}=\{\mathit{v}\in\mathit{R}_{1}\mid\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}\}
, for
\mathit{T}
defined in
Fig.

11
.

Theorem 8.11 (Soundness).

The big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C}_{1},\mathit{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}}\rangle
and the indexed predicate
{\Pi 3}
satisfy the conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
of
Section

7.2
.

Proof Sketch.

We prove conditions only for rule
(app)
, the other cases are similar (cf. proof of
Theorem

8.4
).

Proof of
(lp)
:
The proof is by cases on instantiations of meta-rules.
For rule
(app)

Lemma

8.8
(
4
) applied to
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
implies
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
and
\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{S}_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
and
\bigwedge_{i\in 1..m}V_{i}\leq\mathit{T}
.
Now, from assumptions of
(lp)
, we get

\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
and
\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:\mathit{S}_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
.

Lemma

8.8
(
3
) implies
\mathit{x}{\ :\ }\mathit{S}_{i}\vdash\mathit{e}:V_{i}
, so by
Lemma

8.9
we have
\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]:V_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
.
We can derive
\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{2}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathit{T}
using rules
(
\wedge
I)
,
(
\wedge
E)
and
(
\vee
I)
.

Proof of
(
\exists
p)
:  The proof is as in
Theorem

8.4
.

Proof of
(
\forall
p)
:
The proof is by cases on instantiations of meta-rules.
For rule
(app)

Lemma

8.8
(
4
) applied to
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\,\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
implies
\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
.
If
{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}
we get
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
for
i\in 1..m
by

and

.

(
