) applied to
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{S}_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
implies
\mathit{v}=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}
as needed.
∎

8.4.
{\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}^{\vee}}

A well-known example in which proving soundness with respect to small-step semantics is extremely challenging is the standard type system with intersection and union types
(
Barbanera et al. 1995
)

w.r.t. the pure
\lambda
-calculus with full reduction.
Indeed, the standard subject reduction technique fails
8
8

8

For this reason,
Barbanera et al. 1995
prove soundness by an ad-hoc technique, that is, by considering parallel reduction and an equivalent type system à la Gentzen, which enjoys the cut elimination property.
,
since, for instance, we can derive the type

(\mathit{T}\to\mathit{T}\to V)\wedge(\mathit{S}\to\mathit{S}\to V)\to(U\to\mathit{T}\vee\mathit{S})\to U\to V

for both

\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.x\,((\lambda t.t)\,(y\,z))\,((\lambda t.t)\,(y\,z))

and

\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.x\,(y\,z)\,(y\,z)
,
but the intermediate expressions
\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.x\,((\lambda t.t)\,(y\,z))\,(y\,z)
and
\lambda x.\lambda y.\lambda z.x\,(y\,z)\,((\lambda t.t)\,(y\,z))
do not have this type.

As the example shows, the key problem is that rule
(
\vee
E)
can be applied to expression
\mathit{e}
where the same subexpression
\mathit{e}^{\prime}
occurs more than once.
In the non-deterministic case, as shown by the example in the previous section, this is unsound, since
\mathit{e}^{\prime}
can reduce to different values.
In the deterministic case, instead, this is sound, but cannot be proved by subject reduction.
Since using big-step semantics there are no intermediate steps to be typed, our approach seems very promising to investigate an alternative proof of soundness.
Whereas we leave this challenging problem to future work, here as first step we describe a calculus with a much simpler version of the problematic feature.

The calculus is a variant of
\textsc{FJ}\vee
, introduced by
Igarashi and
Nagira 2007
, an extension of
FJ

(
Igarashi
et al. 2001
)
with union types.
As discussed more extensively by
Igarashi and
Nagira 2007
, this gives the ability to define a supertype even after a class hierarchy is fixed, grouping independently developed classes with similar interfaces.
In fact, given some types, their union type can be viewed as an interface type that “factors out” their common features.
With respect to
\textsc{FJ}\vee
, we do not consider cast and type-case constructs and, more importantly, in the typing rules we handle differently union types, taking inspiration directly from rule
(
\vee
E)
of the
\lambda
-calculus.
With this approach, we enhance the expressivity of the type system, since it becomes possible to eliminate unions simultaneously for an arbitrary number of arguments, including the receiver, in a method invocation, provided that they are all equal to each other.
We dub this calculus
{\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}^{\vee}}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{e}&::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}\mid\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}\mid\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}&\mbox{expression}\\
&&\texttt{if}\ \mathit{e}\ \texttt{then}\ \mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{else}\ \mathit{e}_{2}\mid\texttt{true}\mid\texttt{false}&\mbox{}\\
\mathit{v}&::=&\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}\texttt{)}\mid\texttt{true}\mid\texttt{false}&\mbox{value}\\
\mathit{T}&::=&\mathsf{C}\mid\mathtt{Bool}\mid\mathit{T}_{1}\vee\mathit{T}_{2}&\mbox{type}\\
\mathit{E}&::=&[]\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}\mid[]{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}[],\ldots,[],\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}&\mbox{elimination context}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(field)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}\texttt{)}}}{{\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(new)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}\texttt{)}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathit{e}_{0}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{vs}^{\prime}\texttt{)}}\\
{\mathit{e}_{i}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{i}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n\\
{{{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{1}}/{\mathit{x}_{1}}]\ldots}[{\mathit{v}_{n}}/{\mathit{x}_{n}}]}[{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{vs}^{\prime}\texttt{)}}/{\texttt{this}}]}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}\end{array}}{{\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}={\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(true)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\texttt{true}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{true}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(false)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{\texttt{false}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{false}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(if-t)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{true}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\mathit{e}_{1}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}{{\texttt{if}\ \mathit{e}\ \texttt{then}\ \mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{else}\ \mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(if-f)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\texttt{false}}\hskip 6.47491pt{\mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}{{\texttt{if}\ \mathit{e}\ \texttt{then}\ \mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{else}\ \mathit{e}_{2}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathit{T}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-bool)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}:\mathtt{Bool}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{b}\in\{\texttt{true},\texttt{false}\}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-fld)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-new)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}\hskip 9.24994pt\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-if)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Bool}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{if}\ \mathit{e}\ \texttt{then}\ \mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{else}\ \mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-sub)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{T}<:\mathit{T}^{\prime}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-$\vee$-elim)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{E}[x]:\mathit{T}\hskip 9.24994pt\forall i\in 1..m}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}[\mathit{e}]:\mathit{T}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{x}\mbox{ fresh}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 12.
{\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}^{\vee}}
: syntax, big-step semantics and type system

Fig.

12
gives the syntax, big-step semantics and typing rules of
{\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}^{\vee}}
.
The subtyping relation
<:
is the reflexive and transitive closure of the union of the
\mathsf{extends}
relation and the standard rules for union:

\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathit{T}_{1}<:\mathit{T}_{1}\vee\mathit{T}_{2}}\ \hskip 15.00002pt\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathit{T}_{2}<:\mathit{T}_{1}\vee\mathit{T}_{2}}\ \hskip 15.00002pt\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{T}_{1}<:\mathit{T}\hskip 6.99997pt\mathit{T}_{2}<:\mathit{T}}{\mathit{T}_{1}\vee\mathit{T}_{2}<:\mathit{T}}\

The functions
\mathsf{mtype}
,
\mathsf{fields}
and
\mathsf{mbody}
are defined as for
\textsc{Mini}\textsc{FJ}\&\lambda
, apart that here fields, method parameters and return types can be union types as well, still assuming the conditions on the class table (
\textsc{FJ}1
), (
\textsc{FJ}2
), and (
\textsc{FJ}3
).

Clearly rule
(t-
\vee
-elim)
is inspired by rule
(
\vee
E)
, but restricted only to some specific contexts, named
(union) elimination contexts
.
Elimination contexts are field access and method invocation, where the latter has
n>0
holes corresponding to the receiver and (for simplicity the first)
n-1
parameters.
Thanks to this restriction, we are able to prove a standard inversion lemma, which is not known for the general rule in the
\lambda
-calculus.

Given an elimination context
\mathit{E}
, we denote by
\mathit{E}[\mathit{e}]
the expression obtained by filling all holes of
\mathit{E}
by
\mathit{e}
.

This rule allows us to make the type system more “structural”, with respect to
FJ
, similarly to what happens in
\textsc{FJ}\vee
.
Let us consider the following classes:

⬇

class

C

{

A

f
;

Object

g
;

C

update
(
A

x
)

{...}

Bool

eq
(
C

x
)

{..}

}

class

D

{

A

f
;

D

update
(
A

x
)

{...}

Bool

eq
(
D

x
)

{...}

}

They share a common structure, but they are not related by inheritance (there is no common superclass abstracting shared features), hence in standard
FJ
they cannot be handled uniformly.
By means of
(t-
\vee
-elim)
this is possible: for instance, we can write a wrapper class that, in a sense, provides the common interface of
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
“ex-post”

⬇

class

CorD

{

C

\vee

D

el
;

A

getf
()

{

this
.
el
.
f

}

CorD

update
(
A

x
)

{

new

CorD
(
this
.
el
.
update
(
x
))

}

}

Bodies of methods
getf
and
update
in class
CorD
are well-typed thanks to rule
(t-
\vee
-elim)
,
as shown by the following derivation for
update
, where
\Gamma=x{:}\mathsf{A},\texttt{this}{:}\mathsf{CorD}
.

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{this}\texttt{.}\mathsf{el}:\mathsf{C}\vee\mathsf{D}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\hskip 5.77115pt\frac{\Gamma,y{:}\mathsf{C}\vdash y{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{update}\texttt{(}x\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}}{\Gamma,y{:}\mathsf{C}\vdash y{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{update}\texttt{(}x\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}\vee\mathsf{D}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\hskip 5.77115pt\frac{\Gamma,y{:}\mathsf{D}\vdash y{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{update}\texttt{(}x\texttt{)}:\mathsf{D}}{\Gamma,y{:}\mathsf{D}\vdash y{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{update}\texttt{(}x\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}\vee\mathsf{D}}\hskip 8.24454pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{this}\texttt{.}\mathsf{el}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{update}\texttt{(}x\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}\vee\mathsf{D}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{CorD}\texttt{(}\texttt{this}\texttt{.}\mathsf{el}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{update}\texttt{(}x\texttt{)}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{CorD}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}

The above example can be typed in
\textsc{FJ}\vee
as well, even though with a different technique.
9
9

9

When the receiver of a method call has a union type, look-up (function
\mathsf{mtype}
) is directly performed and gives a set of method signatures; arguments should comply all parameter types and the type of the call is the union of return types.

On the other hand, with our more uniform approach inspired by rule
(
\vee
E)
, we can type examples where the same subexpression having a union type occurs more than once, and soundness relies on the determinism of evaluation, as in the example at the beginning of this section.

To illustrate this,
let us consider an example.
Assuming the above class table,
consider the expression
\mathit{e}=\texttt{if}\ \texttt{false}\ \texttt{then}\ \texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\ldots\texttt{)}\ \texttt{else}\ \texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\ldots\texttt{)}
.
By rule
(t-if)
, the expression
\mathit{e}
has type
\mathsf{C}\vee\mathsf{D}
, and, by rule
(t-
\vee
-elim)
, the expression
\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}\texttt{)}
has type
\mathtt{Bool}
, as shown by the following derivation:

\frac{\frac{}{\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}\vee\mathsf{D}}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\hskip 6.99997pt\frac{}{\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}\vdash\mathit{x}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\mathit{x}\texttt{)}:\mathtt{Bool}}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}\frac{}{\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{D}\vdash\mathit{x}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\mathit{x}\texttt{)}:\mathtt{Bool}}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}}{\vdash\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}\texttt{)}:\mathtt{Bool}}\quad\textsc{myfraction=\hbox{$\frac{,}{l}$} eft={},right={}, vcenter}

This expression cannot be typed in
\textsc{FJ}\vee
, because there is no way to eliminate the union type assigned to
\mathit{e}
when it occurs as an argument.

Quite surprisingly, subject reduction fails for the expected small-step semantics, even if there are no intersection types, which are the source, together with the
(
\vee
E)
rules, of the problems in the
\lambda
-calculus.
Indeed, we have the following small-step reduction:

\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}\texttt{)}\longrightarrow\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\ldots\texttt{)}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}\texttt{)}\longrightarrow\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\ldots\texttt{)}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{eq}\texttt{(}\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\ldots\texttt{)}\texttt{)}

where the intermediate expression cannot be typed, because
\mathit{e}
has a union type.
This happens because intersection types are in a sense hidden in the class table: the method
\mathsf{eq}
occurs in two different classes with different types, hence, roughly, we could assign it the intersection type

(\mathsf{C}\,\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathtt{Bool})\wedge(\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathtt{Bool})
.

As in previous examples, the soundness proof uses an inversion lemma and a substitution lemma.
The canonical forms lemma is trivial since the only values
of type
\mathsf{C}
are objects (constructor calls with values as arguments) instances of a subclass.
In addition, we need a lemma (dubbed “key”) which assures that a value typed by a union of classes can also be typed by one of these classes.
The proof of this lemma is straightforward, since values having class types are just new constructors, as shown by canonical forms.

Lemma 8.12 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma\{\mathit{T}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}\}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{e}^{\prime}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 8.13 (Canonical forms).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathtt{Bool}
, then
\mathit{v}=\texttt{true}
or
\mathit{v}=\texttt{false}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathsf{C}
, then
\mathit{v}=\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{D}\texttt{(}\mathit{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{v}_{n}\texttt{)}
and
\mathsf{D}<:\mathsf{C}
.

Lemma 8.14 (Inversion).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma(\mathit{x})<:\mathit{T}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
and, for all
i\in 1..m
,

\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C}_{i})=\\
T_{i1}\,\mathsf{f}_{i1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{in_{i}}\,\mathsf{f}_{in_{i}}\texttt{;}
and
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{f}_{ik_{i}}
and
\mathit{T}_{ik_{i}}<:T
for some

k_{i}\in 1..n_{i}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
, then
\mathsf{C}<:\mathit{T}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
and,
there is
p\in 0..n
such that
\mathit{e}_{0}=\ldots=\mathit{e}_{p}
and,
for all
i\in 1..m
,

•

{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{i},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{i1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{in}\rightarrow\mathit{T}_{i}
, and

•

for all
k\in 1..p
,
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, and

•

for all
k\in p+1..n
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{k}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, and

•

\mathit{T}_{i}<:\mathit{T}
.

(5)

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{if}\ \mathit{e}\ \texttt{then}\ \mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{else}\ \mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathtt{Bool}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}^{\prime}
with
\mathit{T}^{\prime}<:\mathit{T}
and
i\in 1..2
.

Proof Sketch.

We prove only points
2
and
4
.

(2)

The proof is by induction on the derivation of
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}:\mathit{T}
.
For rule
(t-fld)
, we have
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}
,
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathit{T}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
,
\mathsf{f}_{i}=\mathsf{f}
and
\mathit{T}_{i}=\mathit{T}
, for some
i\in 1..n
.
For rule
(t-sub)
, the thesis is immediate by induction hypothesis.
For rule
(t-
\vee
-elim)
, we have
\mathit{E}=[]\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
and
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{E}[\mathit{x}]:\mathit{T}
, for all
i\in 1..m
, then, by induction hypothesis,
for all
i\in 1..m
, we get
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{x}:\bigvee_{j\in 1..m_{i}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}
and, for all
j\in 1..m_{i}
,

\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{D}_{ij})=\mathit{T}_{j11}\,\mathsf{f}_{j1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathit{T}_{jn_{j}}\,\mathsf{f}_{jn_{j}}\texttt{;}
and
\mathit{T}_{jk_{j}}<:\mathit{T}
, for some
k_{j}\in 1..n_{j}
.
Since
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{x}:\bigvee_{j\in 1..m_{j}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}
, we have
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\bigvee_{j\in 1..m_{j}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}
, hence

\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathsf{D}_{ij_{i}}
, for some
j_{i}\in 1..m_{i}
, by definition of subtyping.
Then the thesis follows easily by assumption (
\textsc{FJ}2
).

(4)

The proof is by induction on the derivation of
{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}}
.
For rule
(t-invk)
, we have
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}:\mathsf{C}_{0}
,
p=0
,
{{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{n}\rightarrow\mathit{T}}
, and,
for all
k\in 1..n
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{k}:\mathit{T}_{k}
.
For rule
(t-sub)
, the thesis is immediate by induction hypothesis.
For rule
(t-
\vee
-elim)
, we have
\mathit{E}=[]{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}[],\ldots,[],\mathit{e}_{p+1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}
, hence
p
is the number of holes in
\mathit{E}
and
\mathit{e}_{0}=\ldots=\mathit{e}_{p}
,
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
and, for all
i\in 1..m
,
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{E}[\mathit{x}]:\mathit{T}
, with
\mathit{x}
fresh.
By induction hypothesis, we know that, for all
i\in 1..m
,

\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{x}:\bigvee_{j\in 1..m_{i}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}
and there is
p_{i}\in 1..n
such that
the first
p_{i}
arguments of
\mathit{E}[\mathit{x}]
are equal to the receiver, namely
\mathit{x}
and this implies
p_{i}\leq p
because
\mathit{x}
is fresh.
Let
i\in 1..m
.
Since
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{x}:\bigvee_{j\in 1..m_{i}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}
, we get
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\bigvee_{j\in 1..m_{j}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}
, thus
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathsf{D}_{ij_{i}}
, for some
j_{i}\in 1..m_{i}
, by definition of subtyping.
Therefore, by induction hypothesis and assumption (
\textsc{FJ}3
), we get

{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{i},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{i1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{in}\rightarrow\mathit{T}_{i}
and,
for all
k\in 1..p_{i}
,
\mathsf{D}_{ij_{i}}<:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, hence
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, and,
for all
k\in p_{i}+1..p
,
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, hence
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{ik}
and,
for all
k\in p+1..n
,
\Gamma,\mathit{x}{:}\mathsf{C}_{i}\vdash\mathit{e}_{k}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, hence, because
\mathit{x}
does not occur in
\mathit{e}_{k}
as it is fresh, by contraction we get
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}_{k}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
,
and, finally,
\mathit{T}_{i}<:\mathit{T}
.

∎

Lemma 8.15 (Key).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{C}_{i}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for some
i\in 1\ldots n
.

In order to state soundness,
let
\langle{\mathit{C}_{4},\mathit{R}_{4},\mathcal{R}_{4}}\rangle
be the big-step semantics defined in
Fig.

12
(
\mathit{C}_{4}
is the set of expressions and
\mathit{R}_{4}
is the set of values), and let
{\Pi 4}^{\mathit{C}}_{\mathit{T}}=\{\mathit{e}\in\mathit{C}_{4}\mid\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}\}
and

{\Pi 4}^{\mathit{R}}_{\mathit{T}}=\{\mathit{v}\in\mathit{R}_{4}\mid\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}\}
, for
\mathit{T}
defined in
Fig.

12
.
We need a last lemma to prove soundness:

Lemma 8.16 (Determinism).

If
\mathcal{R}_{4}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{4}\vdash_{\mu}{\mathit{e}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{\mathit{v}_{2}}
, then
{\mathit{v}_{1}=\mathit{v}_{2}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward induction on rules in
\mathcal{R}_{4}
, because every syntactic construct has a unique big-step meta-rule.
∎

Theorem 8.17 (Soundness).

The big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C}_{4},\mathit{R}_{4},\mathcal{R}_{4}}\rangle
and the indexed predicate
{\Pi 4}
satisfy the conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
of
Section

7.2
.

Proof Sketch.

We sketch the proof only of
(lp)
for rule
(invk)
, other cases and conditions are similar to previous proofs.

For rule
(invk)
,
Lemma

8.14
(
4
) applied to
\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathit{T}
implies

\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
and, there is
p\in 0..n
such that

\mathit{e}_{0}=\ldots=\mathit{e}_{p}
and, for all
i\in 1..m
,

{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{i},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{i1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{in}\rightarrow\mathit{T}_{i}
, and
for all
k\in 1..p
,
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, and
for all
k\in p+1..n
,
\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, and

\mathit{T}_{i}<:\mathit{T}
.
Assuming

\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{vs}\texttt{)}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
, by
Lemma

8.15
and
Lemma

8.13
, we get

\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for some
i\in 1..m
.
Since
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{i},\mathsf{m})}=\mathit{T}_{i1}\ldots\mathit{T}_{in}\rightarrow\mathit{T}_{i}
and
{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}={\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}
, by assumption (
\textsc{FJ}3
) and (
\textsc{FJ}1
),

\texttt{this}{:}\mathsf{C},\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{i1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{in}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}_{i}
.
Assume, for all
k\in 1..p
,
\vdash\mathit{v}_{k}:\bigvee_{i\in 1..m}\mathsf{C}_{i}
and,
for all
k\in p+1..n
,
\vdash\mathit{v}_{k}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, then,
since
\mathit{e}_{0}=\ldots=\mathit{e}_{p}
, by
Lemma

8.16
, we get
\mathit{v}_{1}=\ldots=\mathit{v}_{p}=\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{vs}\texttt{)}
, hence
\vdash\mathit{v}_{k}:\mathit{T}_{ik}
, for all
k\in 1..p
, because
\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathit{T}_{ik}
for all
k\in 1..p
.

Lemma

8.12
gives
\vdash{{{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{v}_{1}}/{\mathit{x}_{1}}]\ldots}[{\mathit{v}_{n}}/{\mathit{x}_{n}}]}[{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{vs}\texttt{)}}/{\texttt{this}}]:\mathit{T}_{i}
.
Finally, we can conclude
\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}
by rule
(t-sub)
, as
\mathit{T}_{i}<:\mathit{T}
.
∎

8.5.
Imperative
FJ

We show here how our technique behaves in an imperative setting.
In
Fig.

13
and
Fig.

14
we show a minimal imperative extension of
FJ
.
We assume a well-typed class table and we use the notations introduced in
Section

8.2
.
Expressions are enriched with field assignment and
object identifiers

\iota
, which only occur in runtime expressions.
A
memory

\mathcal{M}
maps object identifiers to
object states
, which are expressions of shape
\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\iota_{1},\ldots\iota_{n}\texttt{)}
.
Results are configurations of shape
{\langle{\mathcal{M},\iota}\rangle}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{[\iota.i=\iota^{\prime}]}
the memory obtained from
\mathcal{M}
by replacing by
\iota^{\prime}
the
i
-th field of the object state associated with
\iota
.
The
type assignment

\Sigma
maps object identifiers into types (class names).
We write
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}
for
\emptyset;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathit{e}&::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}\mid\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}\mid\mathit{e}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}\mid\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}\mid\iota&\mbox{expressions}\\
\mathit{c}&::=&{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}&\mbox{configurations}\\
\mathit{r}&::=&{\langle{\mathcal{M},\iota}\rangle}&\mbox{results}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(obj)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\iota}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\iota}\rangle}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(fld)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}}{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota_{i}}\rangle}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(\iota)=\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\texttt{)}\\
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\\[7.74997pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(new)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i+1},\iota_{i}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}\\
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{n+1}\{\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\texttt{)}/\iota\}\\
\iota\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i+1},\iota_{i}}\rangle}}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 0..n\\
{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{n+1},{{{\mathit{e}}[{\iota_{1}}/{\mathit{x}_{1}}]\ldots}[{\iota_{n}}/{\mathit{x}_{n}}]}[{\iota_{0}}/{\texttt{this}}]}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}\end{array}}{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{M}_{0}=\mathcal{M}\\
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\iota_{0})=\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\_\texttt{)}\\
{\mathsf{mbody}(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{m})}={\langle{\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n},\mathit{e}}\rangle}\end{array}\\[19.37492pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(fld-up)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}\hskip 6.47491pt{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathit{e}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime}}\rangle}}}{{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{[\iota.i=\iota^{\prime}]},\iota^{\prime}}\rangle}}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{M}(\iota)=\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\texttt{)}\\
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 13.
Imperative
FJ
: syntax and big-step semantics

\begin{array}[]{c}\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-conf)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Sigma\vdash\mathcal{M}(\iota):\Sigma(\iota)\ \forall\iota\in\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})\hskip 6.47491pt\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}}{\Sigma\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{dom}(\Sigma)=\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathsf{C}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-fld)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-new)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-invk)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}\hskip 6.47491pt\forall i\in 0..n}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathsf{C}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}\end{array}\\[11.62495pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-fld-up)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}\\
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}_{i}\end{array}}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}_{i}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}\\
i\in 1..n\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-oid)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\iota:\mathsf{C}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\Sigma(\iota)=\mathsf{C}\end{array}\hskip 13.87491pt\textsc{\scriptsize{(t-sub)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}}{\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}^{\prime}}\ \,\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 14.
Imperative
FJ
: typing rules

As for the other examples, to prove soundness we need some standard properties of the typing rules: inversion and substitution lemmas.

Lemma 8.18 (Inversion).

The following hold:

(1)

If
\Sigma\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
, then
\Sigma\vdash\mathcal{M}(\iota):\Sigma(\iota)
for all
\iota\in\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}
and
\textsf{dom}(\Sigma)=\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
.

(2)

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathsf{C}
, then
\Gamma(\mathit{x})<:\mathsf{C}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathsf{C}
, then
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{D})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathsf{C}
where

i\in 1..n
.

(4)

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{D}
, then
\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.

(5)

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}
, then

\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathsf{C}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}
with
\mathsf{D}<:\mathsf{C}
.

(6)

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}
, then
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{D})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
, with
i\in 1..n
, and
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
and
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathsf{C}
.

(7)

If
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\iota:\mathsf{C}
, then
\Sigma(\iota)<:\mathsf{C}
.

Lemma 8.19 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma\{\mathsf{C}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}\};\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{C}
and
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma;\Sigma\vdash{\mathit{e}}[{\mathit{e}^{\prime}}/{\mathit{x}}]:\mathsf{C}
.

Let
\langle{\mathit{C}_{5},\mathit{R}_{5},\mathcal{R}_{5}}\rangle
be the big-step semantics defined in
Fig.

13
.
We can prove the soundness of the indexed predicate
{\Pi 5}
defined by:

{\Pi 5}^{\mathit{C}}_{\langle{\Sigma,\mathsf{C}}\rangle}=\{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}\in\mathit{C}_{5}\mid\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}\mbox{ for some $\Sigma^{\prime}$ s.t. $\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}$}\}
and

{\Pi 5}^{\mathit{R}}_{\langle{\Sigma,\mathsf{C}}\rangle}={\mathit{R}_{5}\cap{\Pi 5}^{\mathit{C}}_{\langle{\Sigma,\mathsf{C}}\rangle}}
.
The type assignment
\Sigma^{\prime}

is needed, since memory can grow during evaluation.

Theorem 8.20 (Soundness).

The big-step semantics
\langle{\mathit{C}_{5},\mathit{R}_{5},\mathcal{R}_{5}}\rangle
and the indexed predicate
{\Pi 5}
satisfy the conditions
(lp)
,
(
\exists
p)
and
(
\forall
p)
of
Section

7.2
.

Proof.

We prove separately the three conditions.
The most interesting aspect here is that the presence of a memory induces a dependency between subsequent premises in each big-step rule and the hypotheses provided by the soundness conditions are essential to handle such a dependency.

Proof of
(lp)
:
The proof is by cases on instantiations of meta-rules.

Case:
(obj)

Trivial from the hypothesis.

Case:
(fld)

Lemma

8.18
(
1
) applied to
\Sigma\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
implies
\Sigma\vdash\mathcal{M}(\iota):\Sigma(\iota)
for all
\iota\in\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathsf{C}
and
\textsf{dom}(\Sigma)=\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
.

Lemma

8.18
(
3
) applied to
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}:\mathsf{C}
implies
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{D})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathsf{C}
where

i\in 1..n
.
Since
{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}
is a premise we assume
\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}:\mathsf{D}
with
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma

8.18
(
1
) and
Lemma

8.18
(
7
) imply

\Sigma^{\prime}(\iota)<:\mathsf{D}
.

Lemma

8.18
(
4
) allows us to get

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(\iota)=\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}^{\prime}\texttt{(}\iota_{1},\ldots\iota_{m}\texttt{)}
with
n\leq m
and
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}<:\mathsf{D}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash\iota_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
.
So we conclude
\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota_{i}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
by rules
(t-sub)
and
(t-conf)
.

Case:
(new)

Lemma

8.18
(
1
) applied to
\Sigma\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M},\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}:\mathsf{D}
implies
\Sigma\vdash\mathcal{M}(\iota):\Sigma(\iota)
for all
\iota\in\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Sigma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{D}
and
\textsf{dom}(\Sigma)=\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
.

Lemma

8.18
(
4
) applied to
\Sigma\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{D}
implies
\mathsf{C}<:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{C})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Since
{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i+1},\iota_{i}}\rangle}}
is a premise we assume

\Sigma_{i}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i+1},\iota_{i}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
with
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Sigma_{n}
.

Lemma

8.18
(
1
) and
Lemma

8.18
(
7
) imply

\Sigma_{i}(\iota_{i})<:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Using rules
(t-oid)
,
(t-new)
and
(t-sub)
we derive
\Sigma_{n}\vdash\texttt{new}\ \mathsf{C}\texttt{(}\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{D}
.
We then conclude
\Sigma_{n},\iota:\mathsf{D}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{n+1},\iota}\rangle}:\mathsf{D}
by rules
(t-oid)
and
(t-conf)
.

Case:
(invk)

Lemma

8.18
(
1
) applied to
\Sigma_{0}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
implies
\Sigma_{0}\vdash\mathcal{M}_{0}(\iota):\Sigma_{0}(\iota)
for all
\iota\in\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
and
\Sigma_{0}\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}
and
\textsf{dom}(\Sigma_{0})=\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M}_{0})
.

Lemma

8.18
(
5
) applied to
\Sigma_{0}\vdash\mathit{e}_{0}{\texttt{.}}\mathsf{m}\texttt{(}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\texttt{)}:\mathsf{C}
implies

\Sigma_{i}\vdash\mathit{e}_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
and
{\mathsf{mtype}(\mathsf{C}_{0},\mathsf{m})}=\mathsf{C}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}
with
\mathsf{D}<:\mathsf{C}
. Since
{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathit{e}_{i}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i+1},\iota_{i}}\rangle}}
is a premise we assume
\Sigma_{i}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{i+1},\iota_{i}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
with
\Sigma_{0}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Sigma_{n}
.
Lemma

8.18
(
1
) gives
\Sigma_{i}\vdash\iota_{i}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in 0..n
.
The typing of the class table implies
{\mathit{x}_{1}{:}\mathsf{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}{:}\mathsf{C}_{n},\texttt{this}{:}\mathsf{C}_{0}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{D}}
.

Lemma

8.19
gives
\Sigma_{n}\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}
where
\mathit{e}^{\prime}={{{\mathit{e}}[{\iota_{1}}/{\mathit{x}_{1}}]\ldots}[{\iota_{n}}/{\mathit{x}_{n}}]}[{\iota_{0}}/{\texttt{this}}]
. Using rules
(t-sub)
and

(t-conf)
we derive
\Sigma_{n}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{n+1},\mathit{e}^{\prime}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
.
Since
{{\langle{\mathcal{M}_{n+1},\mathit{e}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}
is a premise we conclude
\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
with
\Sigma_{n}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Case:
(fld-up)

Lemma

8.18
(
1
) applied to
\Sigma\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}
implies
\Sigma\vdash\mathcal{M}(\iota):\Sigma(\iota)
for all
\iota\in\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}
and
\textsf{dom}(\Sigma)=\textsf{dom}(\mathcal{M})
.
Lemma

8.18
(
6
) applied to
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}\texttt{.}\mathsf{f}_{i}\texttt{=}\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}
implies
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{fields}(\mathsf{D})=\mathsf{C}_{1}\,\mathsf{f}_{1}\texttt{;}\ldots\mathsf{C}_{n}\,\mathsf{f}_{n}\texttt{;}
and
\Sigma\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
and
\mathsf{C}_{i}<:\mathsf{C}
. Since
{{\langle{\mathcal{M},\mathit{e}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}}
and
{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathit{e}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\,{\Rightarrow}\,{{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime}}\rangle}}
are premises we assume
\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\iota}\rangle}:\mathsf{D}
and
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime},\iota^{\prime}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
, with
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
.
Notice that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}(\iota)
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{[\iota.i=\iota^{\prime}]}(\iota)
have the same types for all
\iota
by construction. We conclude
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\vdash{\langle{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{[\iota.i=\iota^{\prime}]},\iota^{\prime}}\rangle}:\mathsf{C}_{i}
.

Proof of
(
\exists
p)
:  All the closed expressions appear as conclusions in the reduction rules.

Proof of
(
\forall
p)
:
Since the only values are configurations with object identifiers it is easy to verify that the premises of the reduction rules are satisfied, being the conditions on memory and object identifiers assured by the typing rules.
∎

9.
Concluding discussions

The big-step style can be useful for abstracting details or
directly deriving the implementation of an interpreter. However, reasoning on properties involving infinite computations, such as the soundness of a type system, is non-trivial,
because standard big-step semantics is able only to capture finite computations, hence it cannot distinguish between stuck and infinite ones.

In this paper, we address this problem, providing a systematic analysis of big-step semantics.
The first, and fundamental, methodological feature of our analysis is that we want to be
independent from specific languages
, developing an abstract study of big-step semantics in itself.
Therefore, we provide a definition of what a big-step semantics is, so our results will be applicable, as we show by several examples, to all concrete big-step semantics matching our definition.

A second important building block of our approach is that we take seriously the fact that big-step rules implicitly define an
evaluation algorithm
.
Indeed, we make such intuition formal by showing that starting from the rules we can define a transition relation on incomplete derivations, abstractly modeling such evaluation algorithm.
Relying on this transition relation, we are able to define computations in the big-step semantics in the usual way, as possibly infinite sequences of transition steps;
thus we can distinguish converging, diverging and stuck computations, even though big-step rules only define convergence.
This shows that diverging and stuck computations are, in a sense, implicit in standard big-step rules, and the transition relation makes them explicit.

Finally, the third feature of our approach is that we provide
constructions
that, starting from a usual big-step semantics, produce an extended one where the distinction between diverging and stuck computation is explicit.
Such constructions show that we can distinguish stuckness and divergence directly by a big-step semantics, without resorting to a transition relation:
we rely on the above described transition relation on incomplete derivations only to prove that the constructions are correct.
Corules are crucial to define extended big-step semantics precisely modelling divergence just as a special result, thus avoiding the redundancy introduced by traces.

Building on this systematic study,
we show how one can reason about soundness of a predicate directly on a big-step semantics.
To this end, we design proof techniques for two flavours of soundness, based on sufficient conditions on big-step rules.

9.1.
Related work

The research presented in this paper
follows a stream of work dating back to
Cousot and Cousot 1992
, who proposed a stratified approach, investigated by
Leroy and Grall 2009
as well, with a separate judgment for divergence, defined coinductively.
In this way, however, there is no unique formal definition of the behaviour of the modelled system.
An alternative possibility, also investigated by
Leroy and Grall 2009
, is to interpret coinductively the standard big-step rules (
coevaluation
).
Unfortunately, coevaluation is non-deterministic, allowing the derivation of spurious judgements, and, thus, may fail to correctly
capture the infinite behavior of a configuration: a diverging term, such as
\Omega
, evaluates to any value, hence it cannot be properly distinguished from converging terms.
Furthermore, in coevaluation there are still configurations, such as
\Omega\,(0\,0)
, for which no judgment can be derived, here because no judgment can be derived for the subterm
0\,0
;
basically, this is due to the fact that divergence of a premise should be propagated and this cannot be correctly handled by coevaluation as divergence is not explicitly modelled.

Pretty big-step semantics
by
Charguéraud 2013
handles the issue of duplication of meta-rules by a
unified judgment with a unique set of (meta-)rules and divergence modelled by a special value.
Rules are interpreted coinductively, hence they allow the derivation of spurious judgements, but, thanks to the use of a special value for divergence and the particular structure of rules, they can solve most of the issues of coevaluation.
However, this particular structure of rules is not as natural as usual big-step rules and, more importantly,
it requires the introduction of new specific syntactic forms representing intermediate computation steps, as in small-step semantics, hence making the big-step semantics less abstract.
This may be a problem, for instance, when proving soundness of a type system, as such intermediate configurations may be ill-typed.

Poulsen and
Mosses 2017
subsequently present
flag-based big-step semantics
,
which further streamlines the approach by combining it with the M-SOS technique
(modular structural operational semantics), thereby reducing
the number of (meta-)rules and premises, avoiding the need for intermediate configurations.
The key idea is to extend configurations and results by flags explicitly modelling convergence and divergence, used to properly handle divergence propagation.
To model divergence, they interpret rules coinductively, hence they allow the derivation of spurious judgements.

Differently from all the previously cited papers, which consider specific examples, the work by
Ager 2004
shares with us the aim of providing a generic construction to model non-termination, basing on an arbitrary big-step semantics.
Ager considers a big-step judgement of shape
\rho\vdash t\Downarrow v
where
\rho
is an environment,
t
a syntactic term and
v
a final value, and
values, environments and the signature for terms are left unspecified.
Then, given a big-step semantics, he describes a method to extract an abstract machine from it, which models a proof-search algorithm.
In this way, converging, diverging and stuck computations are distinguished. This approach is somehow similar to our transition relation on partial evaluation trees, even tough a different style is used:
we have no syntactic components and
the transition system we propose is directly defined on evaluation trees and corresponds to a partial order on them, modelling refinement.
Moreover, Ager’s notion of big-step semantics is not fully formal, in particular, it is not clear whether he works with plain rules or meta-rules.

Another piece of work whose aim is to define a general framework for operational semantics specification is the one by
Bodin
et al. 2019
on
skeletal semantics
.
Here the key idea is to specify the semantics of a language by a set of skeletons, one for each syntactic construct, which describe how to evaluate each of them.
Skeletons are very much like big-step rules, indeed they can be regarded as an ad-hoc syntax for specifying them.
This syntax is quite unusual, but probably better suited for the Coq implementation which the framework comes with.
This approach is not specifically tailored for big-step semantics:
a skeletal specification can give rise to semantics in different styles, such as big-step, small-step or abstract machines.
However, given the similarity between skeletons and big-step rules, it may be possible to adapt the proof technique we propose to this setting, but this is matter for future work.

Ancona
et al. 2017b
firstly show that with corules one can define a unified big-step judgment with a unique set of rules avoiding spurious evaluations.
This can be seen as
constrained coevaluation
.
Indeed, corules add constraints on the infinite derivations to filter out spurious results, so that,
for diverging terms, it is only possible to get
\infty
as result.
This is extended to include observations as traces by
Ancona
et al. 2018
.
A further step is done by
Ancona
et al. 2020
, where observations are modelled by an arbitrary monoid and a variant of the construction described in
Section

6
is considered.

Other proposals, by
Owens
et al. 2016
;
Amin and Rompf 2017
,
are inspired by
definitional interpreters

(
Reynolds 1972
)
, based on a step-indexed approach (a.k.a.“fuel”-based semantics)
where computations are approximated to some finite amount of steps (typically with a counter);
in this way divergence can be modeled by induction.

Owens
et al. 2016
investigates functional big-step semantics for proving by induction compiler
correctness.

Amin and Rompf 2017
explore inductive proof strategies for type soundness properties
for the polymorphic type systems
F_{<:}
, and equivalence with small-step semantics.
An inductive proof of type soundness for the big-step semantics of a Java-like language is proposed by
Ancona 2014
.

Coinductive trace semantics in big-step style have been studied by
Nakata and
Uustalu 2009
;
Nakata and
Uustalu 2010a
;
Nakata and
Uustalu 2010b
.
Their investigation started with the semantics of an imperative While language with no I/O
(
Nakata and
Uustalu 2009
)
,
where traces are possibly infinite sequences of states; semantic rules are all coinductive and define two mutually dependent judgments.
Based on such a semantics, they define a Hoare logic
(
Nakata and
Uustalu 2010a
)
.
They provide a constructive theory and metatheory, together with a Coq formalization of their results.
Differently from our approach,
weak bisimilarity between traces is needed
for proving that programs exhibit equivalent observable behaviors.
This is due to the fact that “silent effects” (that is, non-observable internal steps) must be explicitly represented
to guarantee guardedness conditions which ensure productivity of corecursive definitions.
This is a natural consequence of having computable definitions.
By using corules, we can avoid bisimilarity, accepting an approach which is not fully constructive.

This semantics has been subsequently extended with interactive I/O
(
Nakata and
Uustalu 2010b
)
, by exploiting
the notion of resumption monad: a tree representing possible runs of a program to model its non-deterministic behavior due to input values.
Also in this case a big-step trace semantics is defined with two mutually recursive coinductive judgments, and
weak bisimilarity is needed; however, the definition of the observational equivalence is more involved,
since it requires nesting inductive definitions in coinductive ones.
A generalised notion of resumption has been introduced later by
Piróg and
Gibbons 2014
in a category-theoretic and coalgebraic context.

Danielsson 2012
, inspired by
Leroy and Grall 2009
,
relying on the coinductive partiality monad, defines big-step semantics for

\lambda
-calculi and virtual machines as total, computable functions able to capture divergence.

The resumption monad of
Nakata and
Uustalu 2010b
and the partiality monad of
Danielsson 2012
are inspired by the seminal work of
Capretta 2005

on the
delay monad
, where coinductive types are exploited to model infinite computations
by means of a type constructor for partial elements, which allows the formal definition of convergence and divergence
and a type-theoretic representation of general recursive functions; this type constructor
is proved to constitute a strong monad, upon which subsequent related
papers
(
Abel and Chapman 2014
;
McBride 2015
;
Chapman
et al. 2019
)
elaborated to define other monads for managing divergence.
In particular,
McBride 2015
has proposed a more general approach based on a free monad
for which the delay monad is an instantiation obtained through a monad morphism.
All these proposals are based on the step-indexed approach.

More recently, interaction trees (ITrees)
(
Xia et al. 2020
)
have been presented as a coinductive variant of free monads with the main aim
of defining the denotational semantics for effectful and possibly nonterminating computations, to allow
compositional reasoning for mutually recursive components of an interactive system, with fully mechanized proofs in Coq.
Interaction trees are coinductively defined trees which directly support a more general fixpoint combinator
which does need a step-indexed approach, as happens for the general monad of McBride.
A
Tau
constructor is introduced to represent a silent step of computation,
to express silently diverging computations without violating Coq’s guardedness condition;
as a consequence, a generic definition of weak bisimulation on ITrees is required to
remove any finite number of
Tau
s, similarly as what happens in the approach of Nakata and Uustalu.

9.2.
Future work

There are several directions for further research.
A first direction is to study other approaches to model divergence in big-step semantics using our general meta-theory, that is,
defining yet other constructions, such as adding a counter and timeout, as done by
Owens
et al. 2016
;
Amin and Rompf 2017
, or adding flags, as done by
Poulsen and
Mosses 2017
.
This would provide a general account of these approaches, allowing to study their properties in general, abstracting away particular features of concrete languages.
A further direction is to consider other computational models such as probabilistic computations, which are quite difficult to model in big-step style, as shown by
Dal Lago and
Zorzi 2012
.

Concerning proof techniques for soundness,
we also plan to compare our proof technique with the standard one for small-step semantics:
if a predicate satisfies progress and subject reduction with respect to a small-step semantics, does it satisfy our soundness conditions with respect to an equivalent big-step semantics?
To formally prove such a statement, the first step will be to express equivalence between small-step and big-step semantics, and such equivalence has to be expressed at the level of big-step rules, as it needs to be extendible to stuck and infinite computations.
Note that, as a by-product, this will provide us with a proof technique to show equivalence between small-step and big-step semantics.

Ancona
et al. 2020
make a first attempt to express such an equivalence for a more restrictive class of big-step semantics.
On the other hand, the converse does not hold, as shown by the examples in
Section

8.2
and
Section

8.4
.

Furthermore, it would be interesting to extend such techniques for soundness to big-step semantics with observations, taking inspiration from type and effect systems
(
Marino and
Millstein 2009
;
Tate 2013
)
.

Last but not least, to support reasoning by our framework on concrete examples, such as those in
Section

8
,
it is desirable to have a mechanisation of our meta-theory and related techniques.
A necessary preliminary step in this direction is to provide support for corules in proof assistants.
An Agda library supporting (generalised) inference systems is described by
Ciccone
et al. 2021
and can be found at

https://github.com/LcicC/inference-systems-agda
.
Moreover, in the paper we lazily relied on the usual setting of classical logic (even though we try not to abuse of it),
however, towards a formalisation, we will have to carefully rearrange definitions and proofs to fit the logic of the choosen proof assistant.

Acknowledgements.

Special thanks go to Elena Zucca, Mariangiola Dezani-Ciancaglini and Viviana Bono for collaborating on this work with many interesting discussions and useful suggestions, which have greatly improved the paper.
